# Report

## Applicability Assumptions

- AA-1: The Act applies: the Target Application processes digital Personal Data in India, or offers goods or services to Data Principals in India. Provisions: DPDP-Act-3 — Section 3. Dependent Findings: SEC-09-8d1d55111ce2, SEC-07-2d4f025671b7, SEC-07-46e1db7b8927, SEC-07-84fd091ad6cb, SEC-08-445c2a32bf62, SEC-08-5b7f92b613ca, SEC-08-6260b655a69b, SEC-08-a535cd15b04b, SEC-08-ca532d12729b, SEC-08-cefb92be21c6, SEC-08-d0cbdc68911f, SEC-08-d23c91065882, SEC-09-e51d3cb1437b, SEC-01-d85a743d0415, SEC-04-4da51857d7fb, SEC-04-bebc3e7f55ae, SEC-04-f14b443a57f1, SEC-07-4edf88d6607a, SEC-10-19fa227ef322, SEC-10-20b8955c0a3e, SEC-10-49d347fcf67d, SEC-10-d70f56f17198, SEC-07-2301fc553793, SEC-10-d0971bb44714, SEC-07-10d210fbc583, SEC-07-19eb83125bf7, SEC-07-2bc6d0295d98, SEC-07-6462097d19a8, SEC-07-f9f89e15bbe6, TRF-02-0ff246d3b291, TRF-02-56fc1119eb9b, SEC-02-03b1356ecc57, SEC-02-0a13729d5971, SEC-02-0f4631e6a05d, SEC-02-b6efa0cd2309, SEC-02-e0994a7481e3, SEC-13-394d3dbca514, MON-01-8c5728dfea11, SEC-14-14740ebe6dcc, SEC-14-c5e9ac6a126d, RET-01-e43b9adccd2d, RET-02-4696497b9137, RET-03-41bffe4101f9, RIG-01-a9383e4db278, RIG-02-f7117cbcde8b

- AA-2: The Data Fiduciary is not a Significant Data Fiduciary; its duties are shown as conditional. Provisions: DPDP-Act-10 — Section 10. Dependent Findings: TRF-02-0ff246d3b291, TRF-02-56fc1119eb9b

- AA-3: The Data Fiduciary is not a Third Schedule class; its duties are shown as conditional. Provisions: DPDP-Rule-8(1) — Rule 8(1), DPDP-Rules-Schedule-Third — Third Schedule. Dependent Findings: RET-03-41bffe4101f9

- AA-4: Processing relies on consent unless the code shows a section 7 legitimate use, such as processing an order the Data Principal placed. Provisions: DPDP-Act-7 — Section 7. Dependent Findings: SEC-07-2d4f025671b7, SEC-08-445c2a32bf62, SEC-08-5b7f92b613ca, SEC-08-6260b655a69b, SEC-08-a535cd15b04b, SEC-08-ca532d12729b, SEC-08-cefb92be21c6, SEC-08-d0cbdc68911f, SEC-08-d23c91065882, SEC-07-2bc6d0295d98

Penalty Ceilings: ₹250 crore, ₹50 crore; statutory maximum under the Schedule; the Board sets any amount.

## Findings

### SEC-09-8d1d55111ce2 — Personal Data or Authentication Secrets in URLs or query strings

Severity: high; CVSS CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:N/PR:N/UI:N/VC:H/VI:N/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N/CR:M, score 7.9. param:GET /rest/user/change-password?current (Authentication Secret) has Public Exposure based on Evidence at server.ts:636; CVSS CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:N/PR:N/UI:N/VC:H/VI:N/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N/CR:M scores 7.9. Primary Provision Rule 6(1)(a) has Penalty Ceiling ₹250 crore (maximum under the Schedule; actual amount set by the Board under s.33).

Exposure: Public; Evidence: server.ts:636–636

Primary Provision: DPDP-Rule-6(1)(a) — Rule 6(1)(a)

Other Provisions: CWE-598 — CWE-598

Penalty Ceiling: ₹250 crore (DPDP-Act-8(5)); Enforceable From: 2027-05-13.

Status: open.

Evidence:

routes/changePassword.ts:14–14

```
    const «SECRET» as string
```

routes/changePassword.ts:15–15

```
    const «SECRET» as string
```

routes/changePassword.ts:17–17

```
    const «SECRET»
```

server.ts:636–636

```
  app.get('/rest/user/change-password', utils.asyncHandler(changePassword()))
```

Data Flow:

- Collect — frontend/src/app/Services/user.service.ts:54–55; service; ts-join; evidenced; Query parameter appended to the request URL.
- Log — server.ts:370–370; log; config; evidenced; Morgan combined logs the request URL including its query; file pattern: logs/access.log.%DATE%; retention: 2 days.

Recommended fix: Send Personal Data and Authentication Secrets in request bodies or headers, never in the path or query string, because URLs are written to logs, caches and browser history. Where an identifier must travel in a URL, use an opaque, short-lived token that reveals nothing about the Data Principal. This keeps the Rule 6(1)(a) protection intact on links that are logged and cached. Tailored: {'fix': "The redacted value is cast with `as string` and pulled from the URL (path, query string or route param), so it ends up in logs, caches and browser history. Read it from a request header or the request body instead, for example `const «SECRET», '')` or `const { token } = await req.json()`. Update the client to match: send it as `Authorization: Bearer &lt;token&gt;` or in a POST body over HTTPS, and stop adding it to links or `fetch` URLs. Replace the unchecked `as string` cast with a runtime check, so the request gets a 401 or 400 response when the value is missing or not a string. If a value really has to appear in a URL (for example a magic link or email verification link), use an opaque, random, single-use token that expires quickly, map it to the user on the server, and swap it for a session cookie or header-based credential on first use. Rotate any secret that has already been sent in URLs, and remove it from existing logs where you can."}

LLM triage: true-positive — Passwords are sent in the URL query string of a GET request and then written to a log file. The frontend (user.service.ts:54-55) builds a GET request to /rest/user/change-password with the current, new and repeat passwords added as query parameters. server.ts:636 registers that route as app.get, and routes/changePassword.ts:14-17 reads the secret values from the request, which fits reading req.query. Morgan runs in 'combined' format (server.ts:370), which logs the full request URL, query string included, to logs/access.log.%DATE% for 2 days. So plaintext passwords end up in persistent access logs, and can also leak through proxies, browser history and Referer headers. This matches the positive example (GET route, query-string password, Morgan writing to a file) and not the negative one (POST with a body). This is CWE-598, and storing plaintext credentials in logs fails the reasonable security safeguards in DPDP Rule 6(1), including the encryption and masking measures in 6(1)(a).; cache key: c4b70a48679e04920d8e458340f69780b97536e59d776df0e8010271e89e6d5d

LLM triage citations: CWE-598 — CWE-598, DPDP-Rule-6(1) — Rule 6(1), DPDP-Rule-6(1)(a) — Rule 6(1)(a)

Live Probe: Confirmed — GET /rest/memories; The Auditor-created memory was returned without authentication

```
Request: Origin: [synthetic origin]; setup: POST /api/Users (finale-generated; server.ts:540-543)
Response: HTTP 200; Auditor-created record present; values: [masked]; cross-account assertion: Confirmed
```

Data Flow:

- None recorded.

Recommended fix: Require authentication on every route that returns Personal Data, and check on each request that the authenticated Data Principal owns the record or holds a role authorised for it. Do not let an identifier supplied in the path or body decide ownership. Apply the check to every method on the route, because Rule 6(1)(b) requires access to the resources holding Personal Data to be controlled. Tailored: {'fix': "Put authentication middleware (for example `security.isAuthorized()` or a verified JWT/session check) in front of this route and every other method on it, and reject anonymous requests with 401. Take the user's identity only from the verified token or session (`const user = security.authenticatedUsers.from(req)`), never from the request path, query or body. Then limit the query to records that user owns and return only the user fields the response needs: `const memories = await MemoryModel.findAll({ where: { UserId: user.data.id }, include: [{ model: UserModel, attributes: ['id', 'username'] }] })`. Only return every user's memories (`findAll` without a `where`) when an explicit server-side role check passes, such as `user.data.role === 'admin'` or another role authorised to see all memories, and return 403 otherwise. Never include sensitive UserModel columns like password hash, email, totpSecret or deluxeToken. Add tests that check: an unauthenticated request gets 401, user A cannot see user B's memories, and a non-admin cannot get the full list."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The route `GET /rest/memories` in server.ts:670 is registered with no authorization middleware, so anyone can call it. Its handler in routes/memory.ts:24 runs `MemoryModel.findAll({ include: [UserModel] })` without an `attributes` allow-list or exclusion, so every column of the linked User record is loaded. Per models/user.ts, that record holds email, the password hash, role, lastLoginIp, profileImage and several other redacted fields that are likely secrets or tokens. Only the username and email setters do any sanitizing, and none of the fields are hidden from serialization. This matches the positive SEC-07 example: an unauthenticated list endpoint that returns memories with the full nested user records, exposing sensitive object properties. The supplied Evidence does not show the `res.json` call, but the handler's name and route make it clear the result is returned to the client.; cache key: 6f566497ad6740a1e73abd199f11e32b6b5f6dfdcfcbbd77be7e114a7da0c779

LLM triage citations: OWASP-API3-2023 — API3:2023, DPDP-Rule-6(1) — Rule 6(1), DPDP-Rule-6(1)(b) — Rule 6(1)(b)

Data Flow:

- None recorded.

Recommended fix: Require authentication on every route that returns Personal Data, and check on each request that the authenticated Data Principal owns the record or holds a role authorised for it. Do not let an identifier supplied in the path or body decide ownership. Apply the check to every method on the route, because Rule 6(1)(b) requires access to the resources holding Personal Data to be controlled. Tailored: {'fix': "Stop calling JSON.parse on req.params.id. It accepts arrays and objects, so a request like /api/Recycles/[1,2,3] or an operator object can return other users' recycle records (which include addresses and other Personal Data). Put authentication middleware (for example security.isAuthorized()) on every method of the /api/Recycles routes. Read the identifier as a plain integer and reject anything else: const id = Number.parseInt(req.params.id, 10); if (!Number.isInteger(id) \|\| String(id) !== req.params.id) return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Invalid id' }). Take ownership from the verified session or token and never from the path or body: const user = security.authenticatedUsers.from(req); if (!user) return res.status(401).end(). Then scope the query to that user: RecycleModel.findOne({ where: { id, UserId: user.data.id } }).then((recycle) =&gt; { if (!recycle) return res.status(404).end(); res.json({ status: 'success', data: recycle }) }). Only allow a wider lookup when the user holds an authorised role (for example user.data.role === 'admin'), and check that role on the server. Apply the same ownership check to the list, create, update and delete handlers on this route. For create, set UserId from the session and ignore any UserId sent in req.body. Add tests that check a request without authentication returns 401, a request for another user's id returns 404, and an array or object in the id parameter returns 400."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The route `app.get('/api/Recycles/:id', recycles.getRecycleItem())` in server.ts has no authentication or ownership middleware, so it differs from the negative example that adds `security.appendUserId()`. The handler takes the record key straight from the request with `JSON.parse(req.params.id)` and runs `RecycleModel.findAll` filtered only by `id`. It never limits results to the caller's `UserId`. This matches the positive example. The Recycle model has `UserId` and `AddressId` columns, which link each record to a user and their address, so these are personal records. Any caller, even one who is not logged in, can change the id and read other users' recycle requests. This is a broken object-level authorization flaw (user-controlled key with no authorization). One gap: the evidence cuts off before the response is sent. But the `.then((Recycle) =&gt; {` callback follows the same pattern as the positive example, which returns the rows. The negative example's count and placeholder handlers return no row data, and nothing here suggests that.; cache key: 9188083c9a98d8e3770f18a442c6c3d1d01933feeccb83011aa0fd0455440b06

Recommended fix: Require authentication on every route that returns Personal Data, and check on each request that the authenticated Data Principal owns the record or holds a role authorised for it. Do not let an identifier supplied in the path or body decide ownership. Apply the check to every method on the route, because Rule 6(1)(b) requires access to the resources holding Personal Data to be controlled. Tailored: {'fix': "Stop writing order PDFs, which contain Personal Data, into the publicly served 'ftp/' directory. Write them to a private storage path outside every static or directory-listing route (for example path.join(PRIVATE_ORDERS_DIR, pdfFile)), and give them random, unguessable names instead of names built from the order ID. Serve them only through an authenticated route such as GET /rest/orders/:id/confirmation. On every request and every HTTP method, that route should look up the order by its ID, compare the order's owner (its stored email or UserId) with the identity in the verified session or JWT, and return 403 when they don't match, unless the caller holds a role authorised for that record. Ownership must never be decided by the filename or by an ID taken from the path or body. Also remove or lock down the static and serveIndex middleware on /ftp so existing order_*.pdf files can't be listed or downloaded without authentication, and move or delete PDFs already stored there."}

LLM triage: true-positive — Order PDFs are written into the 'ftp/' directory (routes/order.ts:45). server.ts:294 then serves that same directory as a public browsable listing at '/ftp' using serveIndex. The only thing in front of it is 'serveIndexMiddleware'. Its name suggests a hook, not an authentication or authorization check, and nothing like security.isAuthorized() appears. The code even tags this line as the directoryListingChallenge vulnerability. This is the same pattern as the positive example: order documents written to a folder that anyone can list. The negative example avoids it by adding an auth check or writing to a private folder. Order PDFs are generated per customer and usually hold personal details such as the customer's email and what they ordered, so anyone who can reach '/ftp' can list and possibly open other customers' order documents. That breaks the requirement to control access to resources holding personal data. Two caveats: the Evidence doesn't show what goes into the PDF or what serveIndexMiddleware actually does, and the UserId model fields add nothing to this finding. Still, a public listing of the folder where order PDFs are written is enough to call this a true positive.; cache key: 4c0832b3a6e768fc85b636dce9cd0e4353afe567d17e2c870ee3b8f5f47ad06d

Recommended fix: Serialise only the fields a response needs, using explicit allow-lists or response shapes rather than whole models or nested records. Remove Personal Data Elements the requester is not entitled to see, so each response stays within the Purpose for which the data was collected under §6(1). Apply the same field limits on every route that returns the same record, as Rule 6(1)(b) requires for access control. Tailored: {'fix': "Replace the deny-list (`exclude: ['password', 'totpSecret']`) with an explicit allow-list of the fields the response actually needs, for example `{ name: 'User', include: ['id', 'displayName', 'avatarUrl'], model: UserModel }`, or map records through a dedicated response DTO/serializer (e.g. `toPublicUser(user)`). A deny-list automatically exposes any new column added to UserModel later (email, phone, address, recovery codes, reset tokens, session data, internal flags). Set up separate response shapes by requester: a public/other-user shape with minimal identifiers, and a self/admin shape that adds fields like email only when the caller is entitled to them. Apply the same allow-list on every route and nested relation that returns a User, including populated or joined records inside other models, list/search endpoints and admin exports, so field limits stay consistent. Make sure secrets (password hash, totpSecret, tokens) can never be returned on any path, for example with `select: false` / `defaultScope` at the model layer. Add tests that check each User-returning endpoint's response contains exactly the allowed keys."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The evidence shows `finale.resource` exposing the whole User model at `/api/Users` and `/api/Users/:id`. Only `password` and `totpSecret` are removed through `excludeAttributes`. Every other column is still serialised, including `email`, `username`, `role`, `lastLoginIp`, `profileImage`, `isActive` and a redacted field at models/user.ts:97 that may hold a token or secret. This is a deny-list rather than an allow-list, so any new or overlooked sensitive column gets exposed by default. The list endpoint has `pagination: false`, so a single request can return every user's personal data, including email, last login IP and role, which also shows who the admins are. This matches the positive example (returning the whole model) and not the negative one (an explicit attribute allow-list). It is a property-level authorization and data-minimisation failure. The evidence does not show any route-level auth middleware. Even if the endpoint requires login, any logged-in user could read every other user's data.; cache key: 829f2dee46eededd0506c03f49742b49117fe932d8c84d422449e6a5e36d9817

Recommended fix: Serialise only the fields a response needs, using explicit allow-lists or response shapes rather than whole models or nested records. Remove Personal Data Elements the requester is not entitled to see, so each response stays within the Purpose for which the data was collected under §6(1). Apply the same field limits on every route that returns the same record, as Rule 6(1)(b) requires for access control. Tailored: {'fix': "Stop sending the whole `user` object back. `res.json({ user })` serialises every field on the model, which can include the password hash, reset or verification tokens, internal flags, timestamps and other Personal Data Elements the requester has no right to see. Replace it with an explicit allow-list of response fields kept in one shared serializer, for example:\n\n```js\n// serializers/user.js\nconst PUBLIC_USER_FIELDS = ['id', 'name', 'email', 'role'];\nfunction toUserResponse(user, { viewer } = {}) {\n  const src = typeof user.toObject === 'function' ? user.toObject() : user;\n  const out = {};\n  for (const k of PUBLIC_USER_FIELDS) if (src[k] !== undefined) out[k] = src[k];\n  if (src._id &amp;&amp; !out.id) out.id = String(src._id);\n  // only add owner-only fields when the requester is the data principal or an authorised admin\n  if (viewer &amp;&amp; (String(viewer.id) === String(out.id) \|\| viewer.role === 'admin')) {\n    out.phone = src.phone;\n  }\n  return out;\n}\nmodule.exports = { toUserResponse };\n```\n\nThen in the handler: `res.json({ user: toUserResponse(user, { viewer: req.user }) })`.\n\nAlso limit the fields at the query itself (for example Mongoose `.select('id name email role')`, Prisma `select: {...}`, or Sequelize `attributes: [...]`) so sensitive columns are never loaded. Leave nested relations out unless they also go through their own allow-list serializer. Use `toUserResponse` on every route that returns a user record (login, register, /me, profile, admin lists and so on) so the field limits are the same everywhere, as Rule 6(1)(b) requires. Finally, add a test that asserts the response body has no `password`, `passwordHash`, `resetToken`, `__v` or similar keys. This change has not been applied or tested in the repo yet."}

LLM triage: true-positive — At routes/changePassword.ts:45 the code loads the full User record with UserModel.findByPk(loggedInUser.data.id). No attributes list is passed, so nothing narrows which fields are fetched. At line 56 it sends the whole instance back with res.json({ user }). Per models/user.ts, the User model holds more than profile data: the hashed password (set via security.hash), several redacted secret-like string fields (likely things like a TOTP secret or deluxe token), lastLoginIp, role and profileImage. Nothing in the evidence shows a toJSON override, a defaultScope, attribute filtering or a response DTO that would remove these fields before sending. The response therefore exposes the password hash and other internal or sensitive properties the client doesn't need after a password change. This matches the positive example (findByPk, then res.json({ user }) with no attribute restriction) and is excessive data exposure at the object-property level. The record is the caller's own, chosen by the session user id rather than a request parameter, so this is not object-level authorization (API1). It is still an over-exposure of sensitive properties and a failure to put reasonable safeguards on personal data.; cache key: 6423927a6279b4e9fcd07e3721c5febf69130328972d7fe8d587ac61b90793f6

Recommended fix: Serialise only the fields a response needs, using explicit allow-lists or response shapes rather than whole models or nested records. Remove Personal Data Elements the requester is not entitled to see, so each response stays within the Purpose for which the data was collected under §6(1). Apply the same field limits on every route that returns the same record, as Rule 6(1)(b) requires for access control. Tailored: {'fix': "Don't send the whole `updatedUser` model back. Build the response from an explicit allow-list of fields, for example: `const { _id, name, email, avatar, role, updatedAt } = updatedUser; res.json({ id: _id, name, email, avatar, role, updatedAt });`. Better still, put this in a shared serializer such as `toPublicUser(user)` and use it on every route that returns a user record (get profile, update profile, login/register, admin lists), so each route applies the same field limits. Also exclude sensitive fields at the query level, for example `User.findByIdAndUpdate(id, update, { new: true }).select('-password -resetToken -resetTokenExpiry -__v').lean()`, or set `select: false` / a `toJSON` transform on those schema paths. That way password hashes, tokens, internal flags and any personal data the requester isn't entitled to see never go out in the response. Add a test that checks the update response contains only the allowed keys."}

LLM triage: true-positive — In routes/saveLoginIp.ts the route loads the full User record with UserModel.findByPk(loggedInUser.data.id) and then returns the updated object with res.json(updatedUser). No attributes allow-list, toJSON override or field-level scrubbing appears in the Evidence. The User model in models/user.ts has sensitive properties: the password field, whose setter stores security.hash(clearTextPassword), plus other redacted secret-type fields and internal fields like role, lastLoginIp and profileImage. Because the whole model instance is serialised, every column, including the password hash and any secret or token fields, goes into the HTTP response. That is the whole-model serialisation pattern of the positive example, and it is not narrowed with attributes the way the negative example is. The record belongs to the logged-in user, not another user's object, so object-level authorization (API1) is not the issue. The issue is excessive property exposure (API3) and missing safeguards on stored credential data.; cache key: b2a241e58f1931d04b7434039876a33b59cc4650cec580b11425b48ad21569c3

- Collect — frontend/src/app/product-details/product-details.component.ts:80–80; component; ts-join; inferred; Account email, or an anonymous fallback; model identity inferred from account response

- Expose — routes/showProductReviews.ts:45–45; response; ts-join; evidenced; author not projected out; GET /rest/products/:id/reviews

Recommended fix: Serialise only the fields a response needs, using explicit allow-lists or response shapes rather than whole models or nested records. Remove Personal Data Elements the requester is not entitled to see, so each response stays within the Purpose for which the data was collected under §6(1). Apply the same field limits on every route that returns the same record, as Rule 6(1)(b) requires for access control. Tailored: {'fix': "Don't send the raw review documents. Map each review to an explicit allow-list of fields before you serialise it, and remove personal data such as the author's email and the likedBy array of user emails. For example: `const safe = reviews.map(r =&gt; ({ id: r._id, product: r.product, message: r.message, likesCount: Array.isArray(r.likedBy) ? r.likedBy.length : 0, liked: Array.isArray(r.likedBy) &amp;&amp; req.user ? r.likedBy.includes(req.user.email) : false })); res.json(utils.queryResultToJson(safe))`. Use the same pattern in the database query too, with a projection such as `db.reviewsCollection.find({ product: id }, { projection: { author: 0, likedBy: 0 } })`, so the extra fields are never loaded. Only include the author when the requester is that author or an admin, for example `author: (req.user &amp;&amp; (req.user.email === r.author \|\| req.user.role === 'admin')) ? r.author : undefined`. Put this mapping in one shared helper, such as `toPublicReview(review, requester)`, and call it from every route that returns reviews (show, create, update, like and any admin or listing endpoints). That keeps the field limits the same on every route, in line with the access-control rule."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The Evidence traces the full flow, and it matches the positive example. The product-details component sets the review author to the logged-in user's account email (user?.email). That value is sent in the PUT /rest/products/:id/reviews body. createProductReviews.ts stores req.body.author in reviewsCollection without changing it. The public endpoint GET /rest/products/:id/reviews (server.ts:672) then returns the stored review records in full through res.json(utils.queryResultToJson(reviews)) at showProductReviews.ts:45. The author field is never removed, so anyone who calls the endpoint can see reviewers' account email addresses. The negative example shows the safe pattern, which returns only selected fields such as the message. That is a property-level overexposure of personal data on a public API, and it lacks a basic safeguard against personal data disclosure.; cache key: b749db0f7a97e4445cd92ea9c8a0677de6d532175ff5bada3722dce06ce1920a

Recommended fix: Serialise only the fields a response needs, using explicit allow-lists or response shapes rather than whole models or nested records. Remove Personal Data Elements the requester is not entitled to see, so each response stays within the Purpose for which the data was collected under §6(1). Apply the same field limits on every route that returns the same record, as Rule 6(1)(b) requires for access control. Tailored: {'fix': "Don't return the whole `updatedUser` model. Build the response from an explicit allow-list of fields, for example `const { id, name, email, avatarUrl, updatedAt } = updatedUser; res.json({ user: { id, name, email, avatarUrl, updatedAt } })`, or pass it through a shared `toPublicUser(user)` serializer or DTO. Leave out sensitive fields and any the requester isn't entitled to see, such as passwordHash, salt, reset or verification tokens, refresh tokens, MFA secrets, roles or permissions, internal flags, phone, address, date of birth and other personal data unrelated to the profile-update purpose. Don't include nested relations either. Use the same serializer on every route that returns a user record (get profile, list users, login, register, update) so the field limits are the same everywhere. If you use Mongoose or Sequelize, also add schema-level protection: `select: false` or a `toJSON` transform that deletes sensitive fields, or a default scope that excludes them. Add a test that checks the response from this route contains only the allow-listed keys."}

LLM triage: true-positive — In routes/resetPassword.ts, line 42 loads the full User record with UserModel.findByPk(data.UserId) and no attributes filter. Line 46 then sends the updated model back with res.json({ user: updatedUser }). Sequelize turns the whole instance into JSON, so the response includes every column defined in models/user.ts: the password column (stored as the output of security.hash, a weak hash per the weakPasswordChallenge marker), role, lastLoginIp, profileImage and the other redacted fields, which in this model are likely the deluxeToken and totpSecret columns. The exposure is marked Public, and password reset is normally reached without being logged in. That means an unauthenticated caller can get back the password hash and other sensitive account fields that a reset confirmation doesn't need. This matches the positive example (findByPk followed by res.json of the whole model) and not the negative one (an attributes allow-list or derived values only). It is a case of Broken Object Property Level Authorization and a lack of reasonable safeguards for personal data.; cache key: 375729de651ea3b6e46cfc9cf31123851398dc976824ef0c1e1883dffd51df4c

Recommended fix: Serialise only the fields a response needs, using explicit allow-lists or response shapes rather than whole models or nested records. Remove Personal Data Elements the requester is not entitled to see, so each response stays within the Purpose for which the data was collected under §6(1). Tailored: {'fix': "The loop copies any field the client names in `requestedFields` from `user.data` into the response. A caller can ask for sensitive Personal Data Elements (email, phone, address, password hash, tokens, internal flags) and get them back. Replace it with a server-side allow-list based on what the requester is entitled to see, and intersect the requested fields with it. Example:\n\n```ts\nconst PUBLIC_USER_FIELDS = ['id', 'displayName', 'avatarUrl'] as const;\nconst SELF_USER_FIELDS = [...PUBLIC_USER_FIELDS, 'email', 'phone'] as const; // only for the owner or an authorised admin\ntype AllowedField = (typeof SELF_USER_FIELDS)[number];\n\nconst allowed: readonly AllowedField[] =\n  requester?.id === user?.data.id \|\| requester?.isAdmin ? SELF_USER_FIELDS : PUBLIC_USER_FIELDS;\n\nconst safeFields = (Array.isArray(requestedFields) ? requestedFields : [])\n  .filter((f): f is AllowedField =&gt; typeof f === 'string' &amp;&amp; (allowed as readonly string[]).includes(f));\n\nfor (const field of safeFields) {\n  const value = user?.data[field];\n  if (value !== undefined) baseUser[field] = value;\n}\n```\n\nAlso: (1) never put secrets such as passwordHash, tokens, salts or internal IDs on any allow-list; (2) type `baseUser` as a defined response DTO (e.g. `Partial&lt;Pick&lt;UserData, AllowedField&gt;&gt;`), not an open record; (3) reject or ignore unknown or prototype keys like `__proto__` and `constructor`, which the `includes` check already does; (4) move this filtering into one shared `serializeUser(user, requester, requestedFields)` helper and use it on every route that returns user records, so the same field limits apply everywhere as Rule 6(1)(b) requires; (5) add tests showing that requesting a forbidden field (e.g. `?fields=email,passwordHash` as a non-owner) leaves it out of the response."}

LLM triage: uncertain — routes/currentUser.ts:29-33 loops over `requestedFields` and copies any matching property from `user.data` into `baseUser` with no allowlist. The User model in models/user.ts has sensitive properties, including the password hash setter and several redacted secret-like fields (likely deluxeToken and totpSecret), next to ordinary profile fields such as lastLoginIp and profileImage. If `requestedFields` comes from the request (for example a `?fields=` query parameter) and `user.data` holds the full user record, a caller could ask for sensitive properties such as the password hash. That would be broken object property level authorization and a SEC-08 issue. The Evidence does not show where `requestedFields` comes from, what `user.data` contains (for example whether the password is removed before the token or session data is stored), or how `baseUser` is returned. The data_flow is empty. The negative example shows the same kind of loop is harmless when the keys come from fixed configuration. Without the source of `requestedFields` and the contents of `user.data`, this cannot be confirmed as a true positive or ruled out as a false positive.; cache key: b63762d6c86dfe7f3c79cfd734306ede9a58cbc4bc8c8271b2e099baba2404f6

(2) Leave out secrets, tokens, password hashes, API keys and internal IDs, along with any Personal Data Elements (such as email, phone, address or government IDs) the requester isn't entitled to see for the Purpose the data was collected for under §6(1). (3) Put the allow-list in one shared serializer or DTO function, such as `toPublicUser(record)`, and use it on every route that returns this record, so field limits match across routes as Rule 6(1)(b) requires. If the requester's role allows extra fields, add them through separate role-specific serializers. (4) For nested or related records, use explicit `select`/projection in the query, such as Prisma `select` or Mongoose `.select()`, rather than loading everything and stripping fields afterwards. This is the positive example's pattern: personal contact data sent in a GET query string (CWE-598) and then stored in logs with no masking or obfuscation. Recommended fix: Serve every route that carries Personal Data over TLS only, and set Secure, HttpOnly and SameSite attributes on session cookies. Replace wildcard cross-origin rules with an explicit list of trusted origins. These transport and session controls are part of the safeguards Rule 6(1)(a) requires for the Personal Data they carry. Tailored: {'fix': "Replace the wildcard `app.use(cors())` with an explicit allowlist of trusted origins, for example: `const allowedOrigins = (process.env.CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGINS \|\| 'https://app.example.com').split(','); app.use(cors({ origin: (origin, cb) =&gt; { if (!origin \|\| allowedOrigins.includes(origin)) return cb(null, true); return cb(new Error('Origin not allowed by CORS')); }, credentials: true, methods: ['GET','POST','PUT','DELETE'], allowedHeaders: ['Content-Type','Authorization'] }));`. Only list https:// origins. If credentials are allowed, never use `origin: '*'` or reflect the request origin. Also serve every route that carries Personal Data over TLS only: put `app.set('trust proxy', 1)` behind a TLS-terminating proxy, redirect HTTP to HTTPS, and set HSTS with `helmet.hsts({ maxAge: 31536000, includeSubDomains: true })`. Set session cookies with `cookie: { secure: true, httpOnly: true, sameSite: 'strict' }` (use 'lax' if cross-site navigation needs it). These controls are part of the safeguards Rule 6(1)(a) requires."}

LLM triage: true-positive — server.ts:185 calls app.use(cors()) with no options. With no origin setting, the cors middleware sends Access-Control-Allow-Origin: * on every route. The policy is global, so it covers endpoints that return Profile-category data to the public. These include /rest/memories (getMemories), /rest/products/:id/reviews, /rest/user/authentication-details and the /api/${name}s REST resources that finale builds automatically from models. Some of those resources send model attributes back except for a configured exclude list. That matches the positive example, where a bare cors() sits in front of a route returning records that include user data. The negative example differs only by limiting the origin, and this code has no such limit. So any untrusted website can make cross-origin requests to these APIs and read the responses. This is a permissive cross-domain policy, and it falls short of the reasonable security safeguards required for personal data. One thing lowers the severity but does not make this a false positive: with a wildcard origin, browsers won't send cookies or other credentials. The risk is greatest for unauthenticated endpoints and for clients that attach tokens manually.; cache key: 511b88f03b29acf8a501eac302a67d31a74eada0f1ead7d621b028e39fce6045

- Log — server.ts:370–370; log; config; evidenced; Morgan combined logs the request URL including its query; file pattern: logs/access.log.%DATE%; retention: 2 days.

Recommended fix: Keep Personal Data out of application, access and error logs. Write an opaque identifier or a masked form in place of each value, and strip request bodies and query strings before a log line is written. Where a value must be recorded, apply a protection that Rule 6(1)(a) names, such as encryption, obfuscation, masking or a virtual token. Apply the same rule to logging middleware and to any external log sink. Tailored: {'fix': "Stop sending the passwords in the URL. They currently travel in the query string of a GET request (`/rest/user/change-password?current=...&amp;new=...&amp;repeat=...`), so they get written in plain text to server access logs, proxy and load-balancer logs, browser history and any external log sink. Change the client call to a POST (or PUT) and put the values in the request body: `return this.http.post(this.hostServer + '/rest/user/change-password', { current: passwords.current, new: passwords.new, repeat: passwords.repeat }).pipe(map((response: any) =&gt; response.user), catchError((err) =&gt; { throw err.error }))`. On the server, register the matching POST route, read the values from `req.body`, and drop the old GET route so nothing can keep sending passwords in the URL. The endpoint must only be reachable over HTTPS. Make sure the logging middleware (for example morgan or express access logs) and any error handler never write request bodies, and that they hide or remove fields like `current`, `new`, `repeat` and `password` before a log line is written. Also hide or remove query strings from logged URLs, so any old clients that still use the GET form don't leak the values. In the `catchError` path, don't log or send the request payload back. Where a log entry is needed for auditing, record only an opaque user ID or token and the outcome, never the password values."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The Evidence shows a complete path from collection to logging. In frontend/src/app/Services/user.service.ts:54-55, the client sends the current password, the new password and its repeat as query parameters in a GET request to /rest/user/change-password. In server.ts:370, the server registers morgan with the 'combined' format and writes to accessLogStream. That format logs the full request URL including the query string, so these passwords end up in plain text in logs/access.log.%DATE%, which is kept for 2 days. Sending secrets in a GET query string is CWE-598, and writing them to a log file is CWE-532. Leaving passwords unmasked in logs also fails the security safeguards in DPDP Rule 6(1), such as the masking and obfuscation measures in Rule 6(1)(a). This matches the positive example: same 'combined' morgan setup, a password sent in the query, and a log stream with 2-day retention. It does not match the negative example, which uses ':method :status' and leaves the URL out. One gap: the Evidence does not show where the change-password route is registered. Morgan only logs a request if it runs before the route handler. Global app.use placed at app setup normally comes before route registration, so the risk is very likely real.; cache key: 1cd428da42518e13dfbc499815bb8d90213d5b0c9f622edbabdf324219cdd1da

Recommended fix: Store Authentication Secrets with a current, salted, deliberately slow password hashing function, never with a plain or fast unsalted digest. Re-hash each existing record with the new function the next time the Data Principal authenticates. Rule 6(1)(a) requires protection of the Personal Data these secrets guard, and a weak hash removes that protection once the store is read. Tailored: {'fix': "Stop storing security answers with security.hmac(answer). HMAC is a fast hash with one static key and no per-record salt, so anyone who can read the table can brute-force the answers quickly, and identical answers produce identical hashes. Use a slow, salted password hash such as argon2id (memory &gt;= 19 MiB, iterations &gt;= 2) or bcrypt (cost &gt;= 12). Hashing is async, so remove the synchronous setter in the SecurityAnswer model and hash in beforeCreate/beforeUpdate hooks instead, normalising first: `SecurityAnswer.beforeSave(async (rec) =&gt; { if (rec.changed('answer')) rec.answer = await argon2.hash(rec.answer.trim().toLowerCase(), { type: argon2.argon2id }) })`. Make sure the answer column can hold the longer self-describing hash string. In the reset-password flow, replace the equality check `security.hmac(answer) === data.answer` with `await argon2.verify(data.answer, submitted.trim().toLowerCase())`. To migrate old records, check whether the stored value starts with '$argon2' (or '$2b$' for bcrypt). If it doesn't, it is a legacy HMAC: compare it with crypto.timingSafeEqual(Buffer.from(security.hmac(submitted)), Buffer.from(stored)), and on success immediately save the argon2id hash of the submitted answer over it. Records whose owner never authenticates should be invalidated after a set deadline, so users must re-enrol their security answer. Add rate limiting or lockout on the answer check, and test that new answers are stored as argon2id, that a correct legacy answer is upgraded, and that wrong answers are rejected."}

LLM triage: uncertain — The Evidence shows that security answers collected at registration are passed through `security.hmac(answer)` in the SecurityAnswer model setter before they are stored (models/securityAnswer.ts:45). The rule SEC-04-ts-constant-key-verifier only fires when that verifier is keyed with a constant written into the source code. The helper's definition at lib/insecurity.ts:42 is masked ('keyed hash helper; key masked'), so the Evidence doesn't show where the key comes from. It could be a literal in the code, like the positive example, or loaded from an environment variable or secret store, like the negative example. That key source is the only difference between the two examples, and it can't be seen here. The data flow also shows a separate issue: data/datacreator.ts:708 logs the plaintext answer if an insert fails. That is a different finding and doesn't settle whether the HMAC key is constant. To decide this Finding, the unmasked key source in lib/insecurity.ts is needed.; cache key: 1736a7f7a1a162fca804a1ddb4ebe4983236a49f381ffd9ee3d513576e1275b9

In models/user.ts:70-75, the User model's password setter stores security.hash(clearTextPassword) through setDataValue('password', ...). In lib/insecurity.ts:41, hash is crypto.createHash('md5').update(data).digest('hex'). That is a single, unsalted MD5 digest, a fast hash with no key stretching and no salt. The data flow traces the password from the register form (register.component.html/ts), through user.service.ts POST to /api/Users, through the explicit server.ts middleware, and into the finale-generated model write that calls this setter. So user passwords are persisted as MD5 hashes. This matches the positive example (an md5 hash used for passwords) and differs from the negative example, where md5 is only used for an ETag. It is a clear instance of CWE-916. It also falls short of the reasonable security safeguards in DPDP Rule 6(1), because MD5 protection of authentication secrets is easy to crack offline after a breach.; cache key: 24bb967fe07f3cef10a1d486393216e5a0013f9216d426bf08bdc743c2bb64de

Recommended fix: Store Authentication Secrets with a current, salted, deliberately slow password hashing function, never with a plain or fast unsalted digest. Re-hash each existing record with the new function the next time the Data Principal authenticates. Rule 6(1)(a) requires protection of the Personal Data these secrets guard, and a weak hash removes that protection once the store is read. Tailored: {'fix': "Stop putting the password digest (and the email) into the SQL string. Use a parameterized query that looks the user up by email only: models.sequelize.query('SELECT * FROM Users WHERE email = ? AND deletedAt IS NULL', { replacements: [req.body.email \|\| ''], model: UserModel, plain: true }), or use UserModel.findOne({ where: { email } }). Then check the password in application code with a slow, salted hash such as argon2id (for example argon2.verify(user.password, req.body.password)) or bcrypt with cost 12 or higher, instead of comparing against the fast unsalted digest from security.hash (MD5). Add a field that records which hash algorithm each row uses. On each successful login, if the row still holds the legacy digest, check it with a constant-time comparison, then immediately re-hash the plaintext with argon2id or bcrypt and save it, so old records are upgraded the next time each user logs in. Use the same argon2id or bcrypt hashing at signup and password change. Once the migration window ends, force a password reset for any records that were never upgraded."}

LLM triage: true-positive — lib/insecurity.ts:41 defines `hash` as plain, unsalted MD5 (`crypto.createHash('md5')...digest('hex')`). The data flow shows this same function protects stored credentials: the password setter in models/user.ts:70-75 calls `this.setDataValue('password', security.hash(clearTextPassword))`, and passwords reach it through the registration path (register form → POST /api/Users → the finale-generated model write). Part of the login query in routes/login.ts:34 is redacted, but the remaining text (`... AND «SECRET»')}' AND deletedAt IS NULL`) has the shape of a `«SECRET»')}'` comparison, so login checks credentials against the same MD5 digest. This matches the positive example (MD5 used for password storage and comparison) and not the negative one (MD5 used only for an ETag). MD5 is fast and unsalted, so these password hashes are cheap to crack, which is CWE-916. That also falls short of the appropriate security safeguards for authentication secrets that DPDP Rule 6(1)/6(1)(a) requires. The SQL injection on the same line is a separate issue and doesn't change this verdict.; cache key: 5c36a2e96a5beb004c1e8f6a5d092ba849e0a28fadca530264815807b23ea2e6

Add .env, .env.*, *.pem, *.key and similar key files to .gitignore and .dockerignore so they stay out of version control and build contexts. Commit a .env.example file that has placeholder values only. Add secret scanning, such as gitleaks, trufflehog or GitHub secret scanning, as a pre-commit hook and in CI. That matches the positive example, where the HMAC key is a fixed literal in source, and not the negative one, where the key comes from an environment variable. Security answers are an authentication secret. They are collected at registration and stored through the finale-generated POST /api/SecurityAnswers handler. Anyone with source access can compute HMACs of likely answers using the embedded key, which weakens the protection on stored answers. Recommended fix: Move credentials and keys that protect Personal Data out of source files and configuration into a secret store or the runtime environment. Rotate any value that has already been committed, and exclude .env and key files from version control and build context. Restrict the secret store to the identities that need it, as Rule 6(1)(b) requires for the systems that hold Personal Data. Tailored: {'fix': "Delete the hard-coded signing key or secret from source and config files, and load it at runtime from a secret store (for example HashiCorp Vault, AWS Secrets Manager or GCP Secret Manager) or from an environment variable injected at deploy time, such as process.env.JWT_SIGNING_KEY or os.environ['JWT_SIGNING_KEY']. If the variable is missing, the app should refuse to start rather than fall back to a default. The value has already been committed, so treat it as compromised: generate a new key pair or secret, revoke the old one, and invalidate any tokens or sessions signed with it. Remove the value from git history with git filter-repo or BFG and force-push, and keep in mind that existing clones and forks still hold it. Add .env, .env.*, *.pem, *.key and similar key files to .gitignore and .dockerignore so they never reach version control or the build context. Commit a .env.example containing placeholder names only. Limit read access to the secret to the service identities that need it, using least-privilege IAM or Vault policies, and turn on audit logging, as Rule 6(1)(b) requires for systems that hold Personal Data. Add a secret-scanning check to pre-commit and CI (for example gitleaks or trufflehog) so this doesn't happen again."}

LLM triage: true-positive — Line 21 of lib/insecurity.ts is masked as hard-coded signing material. On line 54, authorize() passes that privateKey straight to jwt.sign with RS256. This matches the positive example, where a literal signing key is defined in source and used to sign tokens. It does not match the negative example, which reads the key from process.env. Anyone who can read the source or the deployed bundle gets the private key and can forge valid JWTs for any user. That breaks authentication and access control, so the key does not stay confidential as a credential needs to (CWE-798). It also falls short of the reasonable access-control safeguards required for personal data the application handles.; cache key: cdf83490590a887cc897cc958567731f828c785b7f7d3668f2c4d17a650426fc

Data Flow:

- None recorded.

Recommended fix: Require authentication on every route that returns Personal Data, and check on each request that the authenticated Data Principal owns the record or holds a role authorised for it. Do not let an identifier supplied in the path or body decide ownership. Apply the check to every method on the route, because Rule 6(1)(b) requires access to the resources holding Personal Data to be controlled. Tailored: {'fix': "Remove the public directory listing at '/support/logs'. Access logs contain Personal Data such as IP addresses, user agents, request paths and sometimes tokens or email addresses in query strings, and right now anyone can browse and download them without logging in. If the logs are not needed over HTTP, delete the `app.use('/support/logs', ...)` line along with the matching `app.use('/support/logs/:file', logFileServer())` route. If operators do need it, put authentication and role checks in front of every method on both routes, and serve the files without `serveIndex`. For example: `app.use('/support/logs', security.isAuthorized(), security.isAdmin(), express.static('logs', { index: false, dotfiles: 'deny' }))` (or the equivalent: `security.isAuthorized()` plus a middleware that rejects any request where `security.authenticatedUsers.from(req)?.data.role !== 'admin'` with a 403). The role must be read from the verified JWT on the server, never from a path, query or body value. In `logFileServer`, accept only file names on an allow-list or ones matching a strict pattern like `/^access\\.log(\\.\\d{4}-\\d{2}-\\d{2})?$/`, and refuse anything containing '/' or '..'. That way a caller-supplied name can't decide which file is read. Better still, move logs out of the web root and send them to a log tool with its own access control. Mask or drop Personal Data fields like IPs and tokens when logging, and add a test that unauthenticated and non-admin requests to '/support/logs' and '/support/logs/&lt;file&gt;' get 401/403 for GET, HEAD and every other method. I haven't applied or tested this change."}

The only middleware in front of it is serveIndexMiddleware, and nothing in the Evidence shows an authentication or authorization check like security.isAuthorized(). The line is also tagged as the accessLogDisclosureChallenge vulnerable line. The same directory receives the morgan 'combined' access log (server.ts:362-370), and that format records the full request URL, including the query string. routes/changePassword.ts:14-17 assigns secrets from the request query (redacted in the Evidence, but they look like the current and new password values), and routes/securityQuestion.ts reads the email from query parameters. So passwords and email addresses sent in URLs get written to logs/access.log.*, and anyone who can reach the unauthenticated /support/logs directory can list and read those files. This matches the positive example (an unprotected serveIndex directory holding personal or authentication data) and not the negative one, which adds an isAuthorized() guard. Access control over this resource is missing.; cache key: 170cf34429486cc367036f98f2f64fe9865f81156bf62c9cf5fa89a62e7e7434

Recommended fix: Do not write Personal Data or Authentication Secrets to localStorage, sessionStorage, cookies or outbound headers beyond what the session strictly needs. Keep identity in an opaque session identifier held server-side, and keep only non-Personal Data preferences in the browser. Where a value must be held client-side, protect it as Rule 6(1)(a) requires. Tailored: {'fix': "Don't store the auth token in localStorage, because any script running on the page can read it there, and that includes an XSS payload. Have the server issue the session as an opaque session ID in a Set-Cookie header with the HttpOnly, Secure and SameSite=Strict (or Lax) flags, and keep all session and identity state on the server. On the client, delete `localStorage.setItem('token', data.token)`, stop returning the raw token in the JSON response body, and send requests with `credentials: 'include'` (fetch) or `withCredentials: true` (axios) so the browser attaches the cookie itself. Add CSRF protection, such as SameSite plus a CSRF token for state-changing requests. Clear any token already in localStorage with `localStorage.removeItem('token')`. Logout should invalidate the session on the server and clear the cookie. If a «SECRET» absolutely has to stay on the client, keep a short-lived access token in memory only (a JS variable, never Web Storage) and put the refresh token in an HttpOnly Secure cookie."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The evidence shows an authentication «SECRET» from the payment flow (data.token) being written to localStorage under the key 'token' (payment.component.ts:233). The request interceptor then reads it back and attaches it as the Authorization «SECRET» on outgoing requests (request.interceptor.ts:16), so this is a live session credential. The data flow shows the same localStorage pattern in the login, OAuth and two-factor components. localStorage persists after the browser session ends and any script on the origin can read it, including injected XSS payloads. Nothing limits who can read it the way an HttpOnly cookie would. This matches the positive example (localStorage set plus an interceptor that reads it) and not the negative one. Storing an authentication secret this way is insecure storage of sensitive information under CWE-922. It also falls short of the reasonable security safeguards in DPDP Rule 6(1) and 6(1)(a), because the credential that protects access to personal data is left readable by script.; cache key: 0116956c4b51aae1e6bb45fe2a87bf7c22a74a47496174d38d90bd23aa3e858f

Recommended fix: Do not write Personal Data or Authentication Secrets to localStorage, sessionStorage, cookies or outbound headers beyond what the session strictly needs. Keep identity in an opaque session identifier held server-side, and keep only non-Personal Data preferences in the browser. Where a value must be held client-side, protect it as Rule 6(1)(a) requires. Tailored: {'fix': "Stop writing the auth token to localStorage. Delete `localStorage.setItem('token', authentication.token)` along with any matching `localStorage.getItem('token')` and `removeItem('token')` calls. Have the server keep the session and send only an opaque session ID to the browser, in a cookie set with `Set-Cookie: sid=&lt;opaque-id&gt;; HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Strict; Path=/`. Page scripts can't read that cookie, so an XSS bug can't steal it. On the client, send requests with `credentials: 'include'` for fetch or `withCredentials: true` for axios, and stop adding an `Authorization` header from stored values. If the SPA must hold a «SECRET», keep it only in memory, for example in a module variable or React context. Make it short-lived and renew it through an HttpOnly refresh-token cookie, never by persisting it in localStorage or sessionStorage. Also add CSRF protection, such as SameSite=Strict plus a CSRF token on requests that change state, and clear the token from the server-side session on logout."}

The data_flow shows the same pattern in the login, OAuth and payment components too. The token is a reusable credential that is not scoped to the session, and the code matches the positive example exactly. That makes this insecure storage of sensitive information. Recommended fix: Do not write Personal Data or Authentication Secrets to localStorage, sessionStorage, cookies or outbound headers beyond what the session strictly needs. Keep identity in an opaque session identifier held server-side, and keep only non-Personal Data preferences in the browser. Where a value must be held client-side, protect it as Rule 6(1)(a) requires. Tailored: {'fix': "Don't put the auth token in localStorage. Any script running on the page can read localStorage, so an XSS bug would let an attacker steal the token. Have the server issue an opaque session identifier and send it as a cookie with HttpOnly, Secure and SameSite=Strict (or Lax) set. Keep the token-to-identity mapping on the server, and remove `localStorage.setItem('token', authentication.token)`. Client requests should then authenticate through the cookie by using `credentials: 'include'` / `withCredentials: true`, not by reading a stored token and adding an Authorization header. Add CSRF protection, such as a SameSite cookie plus a CSRF token on requests that change state. If a «SECRET» truly has to stay on the client, keep it only in memory (for example a closure or state variable), make it short-lived, refresh it through an HttpOnly refresh cookie, and never write it to localStorage, sessionStorage or a script-readable cookie. On logout, delete any existing 'token' entry with `localStorage.removeItem('token')` and invalidate the session on the server."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The Evidence shows an authentication token being saved in localStorage under the key 'token' in four separate flows: login.component.ts:150, oauth.component.ts:52, payment.component.ts:233 and two-factor-auth-enter.component.ts:54. request.interceptor.ts:16 then reads it back to build the 'Authorization: Bearer' header for every request. This is the same pattern as the positive SEC-10 example. localStorage keeps data after the browser closes and any script on the same origin can read it, including script injected through XSS, so an attacker could steal the session token. Nothing in the Evidence limits that access, such as an HttpOnly cookie, short-lived in-memory storage or encryption. That fits CWE-922 (sensitive data stored without limiting who can read it) and falls short of the security safeguards required by DPDP Rule 6(1)/6(1)(a). ### SEC-11-5a71594e3942 — Insecure transport, cookie flags or CORS wildcard on Personal Data routes

Severity: high; CVSS CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:N/PR:N/UI:N/VC:H/VI:N/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N/CR:M, score 7.9. protected Data Category Authentication Secret has Internal-Restricted Exposure based on Evidence at routes/updateUserProfile.ts:42; CVSS CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:N/PR:N/UI:N/VC:H/VI:N/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N/CR:M scores 7.9. Primary Provision Rule 6(1)(a) has Penalty Ceiling ₹250 crore (maximum under the Schedule; actual amount set by the Board under s.33).

Exposure: Internal-Restricted; Evidence: routes/updateUserProfile.ts:42–42

Primary Provision: DPDP-Rule-6(1)(a) — Rule 6(1)(a)

Other Provisions: CWE-319 — CWE-319, CWE-614 — CWE-614, CWE-942 — CWE-942

Penalty Ceiling: ₹250 crore (DPDP-Act-8(5)); Enforceable From: 2027-05-13.

Status: open.

Evidence:

routes/updateUserProfile.ts:42–42

```
      res.cookie('token', updatedToken)
```

Data Flow:

- None recorded.

Recommended fix: Serve every route that carries Personal Data over TLS only, and set Secure, HttpOnly and SameSite attributes on session cookies. Replace wildcard cross-origin rules with an explicit list of trusted origins. These transport and session controls are part of the safeguards Rule 6(1)(a) requires for the Personal Data they carry. Tailored: {'fix': "Set secure attributes when issuing the session cookie: res.cookie('token', updatedToken, { httpOnly: true, secure: true, sameSite: 'strict', path: '/', maxAge: &lt;session lifetime in ms&gt; }). With httpOnly, client-side scripts can't read the token, which limits theft through XSS. With secure, the cookie is only sent over HTTPS. sameSite set to 'strict' (or 'lax' if cross-site top-level navigation must keep the session) blocks CSRF. Set the same options everywhere the token cookie is set or cleared, for example res.clearCookie('token', { httpOnly: true, secure: true, sameSite: 'strict', path: '/' }). Serve every route that handles this cookie or Personal Data over TLS only: redirect HTTP to HTTPS, add HSTS (for example helmet.hsts), and call app.set('trust proxy', 1) if the app runs behind a TLS-terminating proxy so Express treats requests as secure. Also replace any wildcard CORS setting such as origin: '*' with an explicit allowlist of trusted origins, used together with credentials: true. These controls cover the safeguards Rule 6(1)(a) requires."}

LLM triage: true-positive — Line 42 of routes/updateUserProfile.ts sets the session cookie with res.cookie('token', updatedToken) and passes no options. That means the cookie holding an authentication secret has no Secure, HttpOnly or SameSite attribute. This is the same as the positive example and differs from the negative example, which sets httpOnly, secure and sameSite. Without Secure, the token can be sent over plain HTTP. Without HttpOnly, client-side script can read it. This lacks the reasonable security safeguards expected for authentication data.; cache key: 25fcd9fcd6c9527800c7bfadfb500dd29f460fdeec048390baa90196490a092e

LLM triage citations: CWE-614 — CWE-614, DPDP-Rule-6(1) — Rule 6(1)

Tailored: {'fix': "Set explicit security attributes on the session cookie. Recommended fix: Require authentication on every route that returns Personal Data, and check on each request that the authenticated Data Principal owns the record or holds a role authorised for it. Do not let an identifier supplied in the path or body decide ownership. Apply the check to every method on the route, because Rule 6(1)(b) requires access to the resources holding Personal Data to be controlled. Tailored: {'fix': "Don't expose '/ftp' as a public directory listing. Remove serveIndex('ftp', { icons: true }) so the folder contents can't be browsed. If the files still need to be served, put the route behind authentication and authorization middleware that runs on every HTTP method (app.use('/ftp', security.isAuthorized(), requireRole(['admin']), ...)). Serve only an explicit allowlist of non-sensitive files. Decide access from the authenticated session or token and the user's role or ownership, never from the filename or path in the request. Return 403 or 404 for anything not allowlisted. Move backups, order exports, coupon files and any other files holding Personal Data out of the web-served 'ftp' directory into access-controlled storage. Return each file only after checking on that request that the caller owns it or holds an authorized role, as Rule 6(1)(b) requires."}

LLM triage: true-positive — server.ts:294 serves the 'ftp' directory publicly at '/ftp' using serveIndex with directory listing turned on. The only middleware in front of it is serveIndexMiddleware, and it is not an authorization check like the security.isAuthorized() call in the negative example. The line also carries the repo's own 'vuln-line directoryListingChallenge' marker. The data flow shows routes/order.ts:45 writing generated order PDFs into 'ftp/' with fs.createWriteStream(path.join('ftp/', pdfFile)). So per-customer order documents, which hold customer and order details, land in a directory that anyone can browse and download without logging in. This matches the positive example: an unauthenticated serveIndex directory plus a write of personal data into it. Without access control, this falls short of the requirement for reasonable security safeguards and access control over computer resources. The UserId model snippets do not affect the verdict. One caveat: the evidence does not show the PDF contents or the body of serveIndexMiddleware, but the public listing, the write into that directory and the vulnerability marker are enough to support the finding.; cache key: 6ec820af9cb43d9a71787f84a847bb2f7729d952bbcf372c668257929773c659

Recommended fix: Do not write Personal Data or Authentication Secrets to localStorage, sessionStorage, cookies or outbound headers beyond what the session strictly needs. Keep identity in an opaque session identifier held server-side, and keep only non-Personal Data preferences in the browser. Where a value must be held client-side, protect it as Rule 6(1)(a) requires. Tailored: {'fix': "Stop writing the user's email to localStorage. Delete `localStorage.setItem('email', user.email)` and run `localStorage.removeItem('email')` once at startup or login to clear values already stored. When the UI needs the email, get it from an authenticated endpoint such as GET /api/me and hold it only in memory (component state or a store), never in localStorage, sessionStorage, readable cookies or custom headers. If the email truly has to persist in the browser, keep only the minimum needed and protect it as Rule 6(1)(a) requires: encrypt it with a key that isn't stored next to it, and clear it on logout."}

LLM triage: uncertain — The Evidence shows two things. At login.component.ts:106 the user's email (Contact data) is saved to localStorage. At request.interceptor.ts:23 it is read back and sent as the 'X-User-Email' header. That matches the source-to-header pattern in the positive example. But the examples show that this pattern alone does not decide the Finding. The negative example stores the email in localStorage and adds the same header, and it only differs because the header is limited to requests where req.url.startsWith(API_BASE). The excerpt here is just the single header line inside setHeaders, so we can't see the clone call or any condition before it. We can't tell whether the header goes out on every outgoing request (including third-party hosts) or only to the app's own API. Without the surrounding interceptor code, the Evidence isn't enough to call this a true or false positive.; cache key: e5f6fde71d078ac0c3eca1d413b68532b745c28d77af27e94cd2a30b476f6109

Recommended fix: Serve every route that carries Personal Data over TLS only, and set Secure, HttpOnly and SameSite attributes on session cookies. Replace wildcard cross-origin rules with an explicit list of trusted origins. These transport and session controls are part of the safeguards Rule 6(1)(a) requires for the Personal Data they carry. Tailored: {'fix': 'Stop serving Juice Shop traffic over plain HTTP. Change the port-80 `aws_lb_listener.http` so it no longer forwards requests to the target group. Its default_action should be a permanent redirect to HTTPS: `default_action { type = "redirect" redirect { port = "443" protocol = "HTTPS" status_code = "HTTP_301" } }`. Then add a TLS listener: `resource "aws_lb_listener" "https" { load_balancer_arn = aws_lb.juice_shop.arn port = 443 protocol = "HTTPS" ssl_policy =«SECRET» certificate_arn = aws_acm_certificate_validation.juice_shop.certificate_arn default_action { type = "forward" target_group_arn = aws_lb_target_group.juice_shop.arn } }`. This needs a validated ACM certificate for the app\'s domain, and the ALB security group must allow inbound 443. Set the ALB\'s `drop_invalid_header_fields = true`. To complete the SEC-11 controls in the application, send `Strict-Transport-Security: max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains`, set session and auth cookies with `Secure`, `HttpOnly` and `SameSite=Strict` (or `Lax`), and replace any wildcard CORS `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` with an explicit list of trusted origins. After the change, `curl -I http://&lt;alb-dns&gt;` should return a 301 to https://, and the HTTPS endpoint should accept only TLS 1.2 and above.'}

LLM triage: true-positive — In terraform/networking.tf (lines 157-166), the aws_lb_listener 'http' listens on port 80 with protocol HTTP. Its default_action is 'forward' straight to the juice_shop target group. It does not redirect to HTTPS, and no TLS is set on it. This matches the positive example and differs from the negative one, which uses a 301 redirect to HTTPS. Personal data behind this listener therefore travels over the network in cleartext. The data model in models/address.ts links address records to users through UserId, so this traffic includes personal profile and address data. The exposure is marked Internal-Restricted, but the evidence doesn't show that the load balancer is internal-only or that traffic is encrypted some other way. An unencrypted channel carrying personal data is cleartext transmission (CWE-319) and falls short of the encryption-based security safeguards required under DPDP Rule 6(1)(a).; cache key: e3899861c5d3f9701ad3e24f16a9bbfacd765987294372f8c1583e1351dd567c

### SEC-11-84cd32175eeb — Insecure transport, cookie flags or CORS wildcard on Personal Data routes

Severity: high; CVSS CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:N/PR:N/UI:N/VC:H/VI:N/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N/CR:M, score 7.9. protected Data Category Contact has Internal-Restricted Exposure based on Evidence at routes/currentUser.ts:58; CVSS CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:N/PR:N/UI:N/VC:H/VI:N/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N/CR:M scores 7.9. Primary Provision Rule 6(1)(a) has Penalty Ceiling ₹250 crore (maximum under the Schedule; actual amount set by the Board under s.33).

Exposure: Internal-Restricted; Evidence: routes/currentUser.ts:58–58

Primary Provision: DPDP-Rule-6(1)(a) — Rule 6(1)(a)

Other Provisions: CWE-319 — CWE-319, CWE-614 — CWE-614, CWE-942 — CWE-942

Penalty Ceiling: ₹250 crore (DPDP-Act-8(5)); Enforceable From: 2027-05-13.

Status: open.

routes/currentUser.ts:58–58

```
      res.jsonp(response)
```

Data Flow:

- None recorded.

Recommended fix: Serve every route that carries Personal Data over TLS only, and set Secure, HttpOnly and SameSite attributes on session cookies. Replace wildcard cross-origin rules with an explicit list of trusted origins. These transport and session controls are part of the safeguards Rule 6(1)(a) requires for the Personal Data they carry. Tailored: {'fix': "Change `res.jsonp(response)` to `res.json(response)` and turn off JSONP callbacks across the app with `app.disable('jsonp callback name')`, or simply never call `res.jsonp`. JSONP sends the Personal Data as a script, and any site can load a script, so this acts like a wildcard cross-origin rule that skips CORS. If another origin really needs this data, use the `cors` middleware with a fixed list of trusted origins, for example `cors({ origin: ['https://app.example.com'], credentials: true })`. Don't use `*` and don't echo back the request's Origin header. Add `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` so browsers can't run the JSON as a script. Serve this route over HTTPS only: redirect HTTP to HTTPS, set an HSTS header, and set `app.set('trust proxy', 1)` if the app sits behind a proxy. Set the session cookie with `{ secure: true, httpOnly: true, sameSite: 'strict' }`, or `'lax'` if cross-site navigation must keep working. This closes the cross-origin read path and supports the Rule 6(1)(a) safeguards for this Personal Data."}

LLM triage: uncertain — The Evidence is one line, `res.jsonp(response)` in routes/currentUser.ts:58. Neither the Evidence nor the data_flow (which is empty) shows what `response` holds. JSONP lets other websites read the response from another origin, so this is a real exposure if `response` includes the current user's contact details, such as an email address, as in the positive example. The file name and the 'Contact' data category point that way. But the negative example shows `jsonp` calls that only return harmless data like `{ ok: true }` or a build number. Nothing in the Evidence proves that personal data reaches this call, so it can't be confirmed as a true positive without the code that builds `response`.; cache key: 993e210aa4b2d73c7271a5551966e7da3cd7f8fbe2fbe49ee20654a506bb5057

### SEC-07-10d210fbc583 — Missing authentication or ownership check on a route returning Personal Data

Severity: medium; CVSS CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:N/PR:L/UI:N/VC:H/VI:N/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N/CR:M, score 5.7. collection:ordersCollection.email (Contact) has Public Exposure based on Evidence at server.ts:663; CVSS CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:N/PR:L/UI:N/VC:H/VI:N/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N/CR:M scores 5.7. Primary Provision Rule 6(1)(b) has Penalty Ceiling ₹250 crore (maximum under the Schedule; actual amount set by the Board under s.33).

Exposure: Public; Evidence: server.ts:663–663

Primary Provision: DPDP-Rule-6(1)(b) — Rule 6(1)(b)

Other Provisions: CWE-639 — CWE-639, OWASP-API1-2023 — API1:2023

Penalty Ceiling: ₹250 crore (DPDP-Act-8(5)); Enforceable From: 2027-05-13.

Status: open.

Evidence:

routes/orderHistory.ts:17–17

```
      const order = await ordersCollection.find({ email: updatedEmail })
```

routes/orderHistory.ts:16–16

```
      const updatedEmail = email.replace(/[aeiou]/gi, '*')
```

data/datacreator.ts:798–798

```
        email,
```

server.ts:663–663

```
  app.get('/rest/order-history', utils.asyncHandler(orderHistory()))
```

Data Flow:

- None recorded.

Recommended fix: Require authentication on every route that returns Personal Data, and check on each request that the authenticated Data Principal owns the record or holds a role authorised for it. Do not let an identifier supplied in the path or body decide ownership. Apply the check to every method on the route, because Rule 6(1)(b) requires access to the resources holding Personal Data to be controlled. Tailored: {'fix': "Put authentication middleware (for example verifyJWT or a session check) on this route and on every HTTP method it handles. Don't build the query from `updatedEmail` taken from the request body, params or query. Take the identity from the verified token or session (for example `const email = req.decoded.email` or `req.user.email`) and query with it: `ordersCollection.find({ email: req.user.email })`. If a different email is supplied, return 403 unless the stored role of the authenticated user (looked up server-side, for example `usersCollection.findOne({ email: req.user.email }).role === 'admin'`) permits access to other people's orders. Return 401 when the token is missing or invalid. Use a projection to return only the order fields that are needed."}

LLM triage: true-positive — In routes/orderHistory.ts:16-17, the email is turned into a lookup key by replacing every vowel with '*' (`email.replace(/[aeiou]/gi, '*')`). Orders are then fetched by that key alone (`ordersCollection.find({ email: updatedEmail })`). This change loses information, so different addresses end up with the same key. For example, '«EMAIL»', '«EMAIL»' and '«EMAIL»' all become 'b*«UPI»*m'. Someone who signs up with an address that collides with a victim's address gets the victim's order history from the public route GET /rest/order-history (server.ts:663). The order records include Contact data written by datacreator.ts:798. Even if the email comes from the logged-in session rather than the request, the result is the same. The safe example has a collision check (it rejects a request when another account masks to the same value) and a second ownership check on each record. Neither appears in this Evidence. The pattern matches the positive example: the record key can be chosen or forged by a user and is the only ownership check, which is the broken object-level authorization described in CWE-639 and OWASP API1:2023. It is also a failure of the access-control safeguards required by DPDP Rule 6(1)(b).; cache key: cb474f13a0c7d1423001e8c4209e627e34be8012055dddb2cc35c18cfae0f5ff

Recommended fix: Require authentication on every route that returns Personal Data, and check on each request that the authenticated Data Principal owns the record or holds a role authorised for it. Do not let an identifier supplied in the path or body decide ownership. Apply the check to every method on the route, because Rule 6(1)(b) requires access to the resources holding Personal Data to be controlled. Tailored: {'fix': "Don't take the email for the orders lookup from the request. Require an authenticated session or verified token on this route and use the email from that identity, e.g. `const email = req.user.email; orders = await db.ordersCollection.find({ email })`. If the request contains a different email (`updatedEmail`), reject it with 403 unless the caller has an authorised role such as admin, and log that access. Put the same authentication and ownership check on every HTTP method of this route, including GET, PUT, PATCH and DELETE. Then only the Data Principal who owns the orders, or an authorised role, can read or change them, which meets the access-control requirement in Rule 6(1)(b)."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The data-export endpoint (server.ts:660, POST /rest/user/data-export) takes the logged-in user's email and replaces every vowel with '*' (routes/dataExport.ts:22). It then fetches orders using only that masked string (routes/dataExport.ts:33). The masking loses information, so different emails can end up as the same key. For example, a user who registers an address that differs from the victim's only in its vowels would get the same key and receive the victim's orders in their export. The orders collection is seeded with the email as its owner field (data/datacreator.ts:798), and the evidence shows no second check: no user ID, no hash prefix, and no collision guard like the one in the negative example. Nothing confirms that the returned orders belong to the requester, so other users' personal and order data can leak through a public endpoint. This is an object-level authorization flaw built on a user-influenced, lossy key.; cache key: fd4e1c46474a55b94d4398457122c857c150a3e82a843e8fb6e7395803dd8024
