Tailored: {'fix': "Don't return every row from `UserModel.findAll()` on a route anyone can call. Put authentication middleware (for example `router.get('/users', requireAuth, handler)`) in front of every method on this route so a request with no valid session or token gets a 401. Inside the handler, decide what to return from the identity you verified (`req.user`), never from an id in the path, query or body. If the caller holds an authorised role (for example `req.user.role === 'admin'`, checked by `requireRole('admin')`), allow the full list, but use `UserModel.findAll({ attributes: ['id', 'name', 'email', 'role'] })` and add pagination with `limit` and `offset`, so password hashes, tokens and other fields that aren't needed stay out of the response. For any other caller, return only their own record with `UserModel.findOne({ where: { id: req.user.id }, attributes: [...] })`, or send a 403. On `/users/:id` style routes, apply the same check to GET, PUT, PATCH and DELETE: allow the request only if `req.params.id === req.user.id` or the caller has an authorised role, and send a 403 otherwise. Log each access to Personal Data with the caller's id. Add tests showing that a request with no authentication gets a 401, a non-admin listing users gets a 403 or only their own record, and one user trying to read or change another user's id gets a 403. This meets the access control requirement in DPDP Rule 6(1)(b)."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The route GET /rest/user/authentication-details (server.ts:640) calls authenticatedUsers(), and that handler runs `UserModel.findAll()` (routes/authenticatedUsers.ts:12) with no where clause and no filter by the requesting user's id. The User model holds personal profile data: email, username, role, lastLoginIp, profileImage and other string fields. The route line shows no role check or ownership check, and the context marks the exposure as Public. So any caller who reaches the endpoint gets every user's account record, not just their own. This matches the positive SEC-07 example, which also lists all users with findAll and returns their dataValues. It does not match the negative example, which ties the query to the logged-in user. This is a failure to control access to personal data and to limit which object properties the API returns. The handler's response mapping is not shown, so it is not visible whether sensitive fields such as the password hash are masked. Even so, emails, roles and last-login IPs of all users would be exposed.; cache key: 7424d27a26820bd83d0947355567229d75ad065e81ddca9ac360648fe5cb4922

Recommended fix: Require authentication on every route that returns Personal Data, and check on each request that the authenticated Data Principal owns the record or holds a role authorised for it. Do not let an identifier supplied in the path or body decide ownership. Apply the check to every method on the route, because Rule 6(1)(b) requires access to the resources holding Personal Data to be controlled. Tailored: {'fix': "The route `app.get('/api/Users', security.isAuthorized())` only checks that the caller is logged in, so any authenticated user can list every user's Personal Data. Add a server-side role check after authentication, for example `app.get('/api/Users', security.isAuthorized(), security.hasRole('admin'))`. Build `hasRole` from the role in the verified JWT (`security.authenticatedUsers.from(req).data.role`) and return 403 when the role doesn't match. Never take the role or user id from the path, query or body. Put the same middleware chain on every method of the route with `app.all('/api/Users', ...)` or `app.route('/api/Users').all(security.isAuthorized(), security.hasRole('admin'))`, and the same rule on `/api/Users/:id`. For `/api/Users/:id`, allow access only if the requester is an admin or `req.params.id === String(authenticatedUser.data.id)`. Regular users who need their own record should use a self-scoped endpoint such as `/rest/user/whoami`, which reads the user id from the token. Also strip sensitive fields (password hash, totpSecret, deluxeToken) from responses with a finale/Sequelize `attributes.exclude` or an after-read hook. Then confirm that a non-admin token gets 403 on GET/POST/PUT/DELETE for `/api/Users` and on other users' `/api/Users/:id`, and that admin access still works."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The evidence shows both GET /api/Users and GET /api/Users/:id protected only by security.isAuthorized(), which checks that the caller is logged in but not who they are. There is no check that the record belongs to the caller. /api/Users/:id takes a user-controlled id, so any logged-in user can change it and read another user's record. /api/Users returns every user. The User model holds personal profile data: email, username, role, lastLoginIp, profileImage and isActive. That fits an unscoped authenticated read and broken object-level authorization. One caveat: the handler that runs after the middleware (Juice Shop's auto-generated REST resource) isn't in the evidence, so I can't see whether it narrows the results. Nothing shown limits them to the caller, though, and the POST /api/Users snippet only cleans up registration input. Another caveat: the 'Public' exposure label is too strong, since a login is required. But anyone can register an account, so the risk to personal data stays high.; cache key: cb351980a060f33f62be430f09e1756f4d105d24ee1c701808b7f23a425fcdae

Recommended fix: Require authentication on every route that returns Personal Data, and check on each request that the authenticated Data Principal owns the record or holds a role authorised for it. Do not let an identifier supplied in the path or body decide ownership. Apply the check to every method on the route, because Rule 6(1)(b) requires access to the resources holding Personal Data to be controlled. Tailored: {'fix': "Right now `security.isAuthorized()` only checks that the caller is logged in. Any authenticated user can call `GET /api/Complaints` and get every user's complaints, including their Personal Data. Add a middleware after `isAuthorized()` that reads the user ID from the verified JWT (for example `security.authenticatedUsers.from(req)?.data.id`) and adds `where: { UserId: &lt;that id&gt; }` to the query, so non-privileged users only get their own complaints. Only users with an authorised role, such as `security.isAccounting()` or an admin role check, should get the unfiltered list. Never take ownership from a `UserId` sent in the query, path or body. Apply the same check to every other method on the route (`GET /api/Complaints/:id`, `PUT`, `PATCH` and `DELETE`). For record-level routes, load the record and return 403 if its `UserId` doesn't match the token's user ID and the caller has no authorised role. For `POST`, set `UserId` from the token on the server and ignore any value the client sends. Then add tests confirming that user A gets only A's complaints, gets 403 on B's complaint by ID, and gets 401 without a token."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The route GET /api/Complaints is protected only by security.isAuthorized(), which checks that the caller is logged in but not who they are. The handler is the auto-generated finale.resource list endpoint (endpoints `/api/${name}s` and `/api/${name}s/:id`, pagination: false). No ownership filter appears anywhere, such as scoping results to the caller's UserId. As a result, any logged-in user can read every user's complaint records. Those records include UserId, a free-text message (up to 4096 characters) and an uploaded file reference, which is personal data tied to identifiable users. The supplied Evidence shows no object-level or owner-level authorization check, so this fits the rule for an unscoped authenticated read. The /api/Users registration code in the Evidence does not affect this finding. The DPDP Act Section 18 and Section 28 provisions are about the Data Protection Board's own complaint process, not this access-control failure, so they are not cited.; cache key: 290d02691931277d34f9c78540652f1f10a1ca0fe9f87d99d65721c530c3a136

Status: Needs Legal Review.

Legal reading — DPDP-Act-16(1) — Section 16(1): Transfer is lawful unless the destination is restricted by notification; this region is a deployment default, not evidence of deployed geography.

Legal reading — DPDP-Act-11(1)(b) — Section 11(1)(b): The Data Principal may be owed a summary of evidenced sharing; configured storage alone does not establish sharing.

Recommended fix: Record the destination of each outbound transfer of Personal Data, and check it against any restriction the Central Government has notified under §16(1). Rule 15 governs data made available to foreign States, and Rule 13(4) applies where a Significant Data Fiduciary is involved. Send any flow whose destination is not restricted to legal review, and state the destination in the notice. Tailored: {'fix': 'The default `aws_region = "eu-west-1"` means Personal Data goes to AWS Ireland (EU), outside India, unless someone overrides it. Treat this as an outbound transfer under DPDP §16. 1) Record it in the data-transfer register: destination country Ireland (AWS eu-west-1), the categories of Personal Data stored or processed there, the purpose, and the processor (AWS). 2) Check Ireland against the Central Government\'s current §16(1) notifications. If it is restricted, stop the deployment and move to an allowed region such as ap-south-1 (Mumbai). If it is not restricted, send the flow to legal review as the guidance requires, and have legal confirm any Rule 15 duties on data made available to foreign States and any Rule 13(4) duties if the Fiduciary is a Significant Data Fiduciary. 3) Stop the destination from being chosen silently: remove the default or set it to an approved in-country region, and add a `validation` block that only accepts regions on a legally approved allowlist, for example `validation { condition = contains(var.approved_regions, var.aws_region) error_message = "Region not approved for Personal Data transfer under DPDP §16(1)." }`. Any new region should then need legal sign-off. 4) Name the destination (Ireland / EU, AWS eu-west-1) in the privacy notice given to Data Principals, and update the notice whenever the region changes.'}

LLM triage: true-positive — The Terraform AWS provider uses var.aws_region, and its default is "eu-west-1" (Ireland), which is outside India. The EFS file system aws_efs_file_system.juice_shop_data is tagged "${var.project_name}-sqlite-data", so it is the store for the application's SQLite database. The data flow shows users' email addresses (Contact data) collected through the registration form, sent to POST /api/Users and saved through the Sequelize User model (models/user.ts email field). So personal data collected from users ends up stored in a region outside India, which is a cross-border transfer under Section 16 and Rule 15. This matches the positive example exactly (eu-west-1 default, EFS, Sequelize User email). The negative example only differs by using ap-south-1. Two caveats: the evidence doesn't show whether a tfvars file or environment variable overrides the region, and Section 16 only blocks transfers to countries the government has notified. Neither one removes the transfer this rule is meant to flag.; cache key: d17a36be8a7454beb8a0490e96c398fb5e3d06f3a0eee242401395b2bce9f47d

LLM triage citations: DPDP-Act-16 — Section 16, DPDP-Act-16(1) — Section 16(1), DPDP-Rule-15 — Rule 15

Any new region should then need legal sign-off. 4) Name the destination (Ireland / EU, AWS eu-west-1) in the privacy notice given to Data Principals, and update the notice whenever the region changes.'}

LLM triage: true-positive — The Evidence shows contact data being stored outside India. The AWS provider takes its region from var.aws_region, which defaults to "eu-west-1" (Ireland), and no other region setting appears in the Evidence. The EFS file system "juice_shop_data" is tagged "${var.project_name}-sqlite-data", so it holds the app's SQLite database. The data flow shows user emails being collected at registration (register form, then user.service POST, then POST /api/Users) and saved through the Sequelize User model's email field. That puts the stored contact data in an EU region, which is a cross-border transfer. This matches the positive example (eu-west-1 default, EFS, Sequelize User email) and not the negative one (ap-south-1). Under Section 16(1) and Rule 15, transfers outside India are allowed unless the Central Government restricts them, and they must meet any requirements it specifies. So the finding is a real cross-border transfer that needs a compliance check, not necessarily a breach. Caveats: no tfvars or other override is shown that could change the region, and none of the Section 17 exemptions apply on this Evidence.; cache key: 1b8539d6bd22a8735ba10b86b710e5b0193bfffd83f1c258ba2f58866a2a08ea

Tailored: {'fix': "The evidence shows only `SecurityQuestionId`, an integer foreign key that points to a shared catalogue of standard security questions. On its own it is not a Tier A or Tier B Personal Data Element, and a database dump would show only which standard question a user picked. Don't encrypt this column: that would break the foreign-key join to SecurityQuestions and add almost no protection. Focus SEC-02 on the related `answer` column in the same SecurityAnswer model. Before the value is stored, normalise the answer (trim and lowercase), then hash it with a keyed hash such as HMAC-SHA256. A slow per-record salted hash like Argon2id or bcrypt works too, as long as it also uses a pepper. Keep the HMAC key or pepper outside the database, for example in a KMS or HSM, a secrets manager, or an environment secret that is never written to the DB or its backups. Do not use a hard-coded key in the source code, and do not use reversible encryption, because answers only ever need to be checked, never read back. Check answers by recomputing the hash and comparing in constant time. Rotate the key, and require users to re-enroll their answers if the key is compromised. Treat disk or volume encryption as defence in depth only, not as the control that clears this Finding. If your data inventory classifies the chosen question as personal data, protect `SecurityQuestionId` with deterministic field-level encryption, again with the key held outside the database. Otherwise, mark this specific Finding as not applicable to the ID column and record the answer column as the protected element under Rule 6(1)(a) and IT Act §43A."}

LLM triage: false-positive — The flagged field, SecurityQuestionId (models/securityAnswer.ts:33-35), is an INTEGER foreign key. It only records which predefined security question the user picked. It is not the authentication secret. The secret is the answer, and that is a separate attribute (datacreator.ts:707 creates the record with { SecurityQuestionId, UserId, answer }). The data flow also shows the chosen question is meant to be shown to users: routes/securityQuestion.ts returns it as part of the password-reset flow. So encrypting or masking this ID would not protect any secret. Encrypting a foreign key would also break the relational join for no security gain. The positive example flags a sensitive value (a card number) stored as a plain integer. This case is different: it's a non-secret reference ID. The Terraform EFS and SQLite storage snippets are general storage context and don't show that this field holds sensitive authentication data. Missing-encryption rules (CWE-311, DPDP Rule 6(1)(a)) don't apply to this field. Any real concern would be about how the 'answer' attribute is stored, and that is not what this finding flags.; cache key: 11bbd75f32407dd60249a8cf4d16735140a035f34157ba2cc37538edbd06fc0a

Disk or volume encryption protects only the storage medium, so it lowers severity without clearing the Finding. Until DPDP §44(2) omits IT Act §43A, that section's reasonable-security duty applies alongside Rule 6(1)(a). Tailored: {'fix': "Stop storing the card number in plaintext. The `cardNum` column is a Tier A Personal Data Element (a payment card PAN), and as written it ends up readable in any database dump or restored backup. The best fix is to not store the PAN at all. Send it straight from the client to a PCI-DSS compliant payment gateway or vault (for example Razorpay, Stripe or Juspay tokenization) and keep only what comes back: the gateway token, the last 4 digits, the card brand and the expiry month/year. Replace `cardNum` with something like `«SECRET» type: DataTypes.STRING, allowNull: false }` and `cardLast4: { type: DataTypes.STRING(4), validate: { is: /^\\d{4}$/ } }`. If the business truly has to keep the PAN, encrypt it in the application before it reaches the database. Use AES-256-GCM with envelope encryption: get a data key from an external KMS or HSM (AWS KMS, GCP KMS, Azure Key Vault or HashiCorp Vault), and never keep keys in the database, the repo or the same host's `.env`. Store the PAN as a `DataTypes.STRING` or `BLOB` holding `iv \|\| ciphertext \|\| authTag \|\| keyId`, encrypting in a `beforeCreate`/`beforeUpdate` hook or a custom setter and decrypting only on authorized code paths. If you need to look cards up, add a separate `cardNumHmac` column (HMAC-SHA256 with a separate KMS-held key). Also fix the type: a 16-digit number doesn't fit in `DataTypes.INTEGER` (32-bit), and a PAN shouldn't be numeric anyway because leading zeros and PANs of 12 to 19 digits get lost. Before tokenizing or encrypting, validate it as a 12 to 19 digit string with a Luhn check instead of the `min`/`max` integer range. Then write a migration that encrypts or tokenizes the existing rows and drops the plaintext column. Rotate keys, purge or re-encrypt old backups and replicas that still hold plaintext PANs, keep PANs out of logs, error messages and API responses (show a masked `**** **** **** 1234`), and restrict decrypt permission through KMS IAM policy. Disk or TDE encryption can stay as defense in depth, but it doesn't close this finding. This meets DPDP Rule 6(1)(a), IT Act §43A reasonable security practices and PCI-DSS Req. 3.4/3.5. I haven't run or tested any of this against your codebase."}

LLM triage: true-positive — In models/card.ts:38-45, cardNum is a plain Sequelize DataTypes.INTEGER field. Its only constraints are isInt/min/max validators. There is no setter, hook or encryption/tokenization transform, so this matches the positive example and not the negative one, which uses encrypt() in a setter. data/datacreator.ts:271-277 writes Number(card.cardNum) directly through CardModel.create, and the finale-generated POST /api/Cards handler writes user-submitted card numbers the same way. Together these confirm that full card numbers are stored in plaintext in the SQLite database (models/index.ts:42, data/juiceshop.sqlite). The masking in routes/payment.ts:32,58 only affects API responses, not what is stored. The EFS volume holding the SQLite file has encrypted = var.efs_encrypted, so disk encryption depends on a variable whose value isn't shown. Even if it is enabled, it only protects the volume. It does not protect the field from anyone who can read the database through the app or a database connection. Financial card data is therefore stored without field-level encryption, masking or tokens, which is the safeguard Rule 6(1)(a) describes and the weakness CWE-311 names.; cache key: 6811b16adf6548b184afebde12c06939d52ece8f36025726ed22ac7612213029

Recommended fix: Protect each Tier A and Tier B Personal Data Element at field or column level before it is stored, with keys held outside the data store, so that a database dump or a restored backup does not reveal the values. Disk or volume encryption protects only the storage medium, so it lowers severity without clearing the Finding. Until DPDP §44(2) omits IT Act §43A, that section's reasonable-security duty applies alongside Rule 6(1)(a). Tailored: {'fix': "The security answer is already protected at field level before storage, because the model setter replaces it with security.hmac(answer). The weak spot is the key and the hash type. In the standard version of this code base (OWASP Juice Shop, lib/insecurity.ts), security.hmac uses a fast HMAC-SHA256 with a fixed key written into the source code. I can't see lib/insecurity.ts in the evidence, so check it to confirm. If it holds, anyone with a database dump can read the key from the code and quickly guess the answers, which are short and easy to predict. That means a dump or restored backup can still reveal them. Recommended fix: (1) Remove the fixed key. Load a high-entropy secret (a 'pepper') at runtime from a KMS, HSM or secrets manager, never from the source code or the database, and rotate it with a version ID stored next to each hash. (2) The answer only ever needs to be checked, never read back, so replace the plain HMAC with a slow, salted one-way hash. For example: argon2id(normalize(answer), per-record random salt), then HMAC-SHA256 the result with the pepper, or encrypt it with AES-GCM using a KMS-managed key. Normalize before hashing (trim, lowercase, Unicode NFKC) so correct answers still match. Store the salt and key version, not the plaintext. (3) Check answers with argon2.verify plus a constant-time comparison of the pepper step. Never compare by recomputing a deterministic value in a database WHERE clause. (4) Migration: treat existing hashes as compromised, because the old key is public. Make users re-enroll their security answer, or set a flag that forces a reset, and do not rehash the old values. (5) Add a test checking that the stored column holds no plaintext or old-style HMAC values and that no key appears in the repo or database. Also add a secret-scanning rule for the old key. Disk or volume encryption alone does not close this finding. Tie the control to DPDP Rule 6(1)(a) and to IT Act §43A's reasonable-security duty, which still applies until DPDP §44(2) repeals it."}

LLM triage: false-positive — The rule SEC-02-sequelize-plain-fields flags Sequelize fields that store sensitive data as plain values. The evidence shows the opposite for this field. In models/securityAnswer.ts:42-47, the `answer` attribute has a custom setter, `set (answer) { this.setDataValue('answer', security.hmac(answer)) }`, so every write is HMAC-transformed before it is persisted. This applies to both store paths in the data flow: the finale-generated POST /api/SecurityAnswers handler and SecurityAnswerModel.create in data/datacreator.ts:707. This matches the supplied negative example, where a setter transforms the value before setDataValue, and it counts as obfuscation under the safeguards in Rule 6(1)(a). The other evidence does not show plain storage of this field. The SQLite path (models/index.ts:42) and the EFS volume (terraform/networking.tf, whose encryption depends on var.efs_encrypted, which is not shown) describe where the data is stored, but the stored column value is still an HMAC digest. Separate concerns exist but fall outside this rule: data/datacreator.ts:708 logs the plaintext answer in an error message, and the strength of the HMAC key is not shown in the evidence. These should be triaged under a logging or key-management rule, not as a plain-field storage finding.; cache key: a392c8f514f76f0e7f28ef9ccb66c7345b460c81b5d510f9bcd074f29501596a

- Log — server.ts:370–370; log; config; evidenced; Morgan combined logs the request URL including its query; file pattern: logs/access.log.%DATE%; retention: 2 days.

Recommended fix: Keep access and processing logs for Personal Data for at least one year, as Rule 6(1)(e) and Rule 8(3) require, and set that period in the log store's own retention configuration. Where an erasure would remove records that these logs must keep, send the conflict to legal review rather than deleting the logs. Confirm the retention setting in deployment configuration as well as in application code. Tailored: {'fix': "Access logs are deleted after 2 days because of `max_logs: '2d'`. Rule 6(1)(e) and Rule 8(3) require access and processing logs for Personal Data to be kept for at least one year. Change the stream config to `max_logs: '366d'` (or longer, such as '400d', to leave a margin), and keep `frequency: 'daily'` and `date_format: 'YYYY-MM-DD'` so each file covers one day. Make the period configurable, for example `max_logs: process.env.ACCESS_LOG_RETENTION \|\| '366d'`, and fail at startup if the value is under 365 days. Store `logs/` and `logs/audit.json` on persistent storage, not an ephemeral container filesystem, so restarts and redeploys don't wipe them. Check the deployment configuration too: logrotate rules, container volume settings, and any log shipping or retention policy on the sink (such as CloudWatch, ELK or Loki) must also keep logs for at least 365 days and must not override the application setting with a shorter one. Don't let erasure requests delete or edit these logs. If an erasure would remove records the logs must keep, flag it for legal review instead."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The Evidence shows the same pattern as the positive example. server.ts:362-369 sets up a daily rotating access log stream (logs/access.log.%DATE%) with max_logs: '2d', so access logs are deleted after 2 days. server.ts:370 sends Morgan 'combined' output to that stream, and this format records the full request URL, including the query string. frontend/src/app/Services/user.service.ts:54-55 sends the current, new and repeat passwords as query parameters in a GET to /rest/user/change-password. Those authentication secrets therefore end up in the access logs, which is sensitive information written to a log file. Keeping traffic and processing logs for only 2 days also falls well short of the one-year minimum in DPDP Rule 6(1)(e), which requires logs for detecting and investigating unauthorised access, and Rule 8(3), which requires keeping associated traffic data and logs. Both problems are real: the short retention breaks the logging rules, and the credentials should not be in the URL or the logs at all.; cache key: 520e641a1b4c3341046c0c1603089592346be669f6f7ad6ad9dd2d726b067b17

Recommended fix: Set up alerting or scheduled review on the access logs and authentication events for every store that holds Personal Data, so unauthorised access is detected and its cause and impact can be established. Rule 6(1)(c) requires monitoring and review of this kind, and investigation depends on it. Make sure the alert rules cover authentication failures and privileged reads, not only application errors. Tailored: {'fix': 'No code change is needed for this line. The evidence is a templated UI or achievement title string ("Solved $difficulties Star Challenges"). It does not access, store or log Personal Data, and it is not a monitoring setting, so it cannot show a MON-01 gap. Mark this finding as a false positive. To actually check MON-01, look at the data stores and auth layer instead, such as the user or customer database, login and session handlers, and admin or privileged read endpoints. Confirm that authentication failures, successful logins, privilege changes and privileged reads of Personal Data are logged in a structured, tamper-resistant form. Those logs should go to a central system with alert rules or a scheduled review covering failed-login spikes, unusual privileged reads and access outside normal patterns, as Rule 6(1)(c) requires.'}

Data Flow:

- Store — data/datacreator.ts:253–262; model; semgrep:op-store-sequelize-create; evidenced; UserId referenced in $VAR

Recommended fix: Back up every persistent store that holds Personal Data on a schedule, with encrypted copies held apart from the primary store. Test restoration before relying on the backups, and record the recovery point and recovery time each store must meet. Rule 6(1)(d) requires appropriate measures to continue processing if the data is compromised, and tested backups are the usual means of meeting it. Tailored: {'fix': 'The EFS file system `juice_shop_data` holds the Juice Shop SQLite data. That data is Personal Data, and nothing backs it up. Encryption is also left to a variable. To fix this: (1) Set `encrypted = true` and `kms_key_id = aws_kms_key.efs.arn` on `aws_efs_file_system.juice_shop_data`, using a customer-managed key, instead of relying on `var.efs_encrypted`. Or add a variable validation that rejects false. (2) Turn on backups with `resource "aws_efs_backup_policy" "juice_shop_data" { file_system_id = aws_efs_file_system.juice_shop_data.id\n backup_policy { status = "ENABLED" } }`. (3) Add an explicit AWS Backup plan so the schedule and the separate copy are defined in code. Create an `aws_backup_vault` (primary) encrypted with its own KMS key. Create a second `aws_backup_vault` in a different region or a dedicated backup account, with its own KMS key and an `aws_backup_vault_lock_configuration` so copies can\'t be changed or deleted. Create an `aws_backup_plan` with a rule such as `schedule = "cron(0 */6 * * ? *)"` (pick the interval to match your RPO), `lifecycle { delete_after = 35 }`, and a `copy_action { destination_vault_arn = &lt;secondary vault arn&gt;; lifecycle { delete_after = 35 } }`. Then add an `aws_backup_selection` that targets `aws_efs_file_system.juice_shop_data.arn`, with an IAM role that has the AWSBackupServiceRolePolicyForBackup and ...ForRestores policies attached. (4) Write down the recovery targets for this store in the variables and the runbook, for example `var.sqlite_rpo_hours = 6` and `var.sqlite_rto_hours = 4`, and make the backup schedule match the RPO. (5) Test restores. Use `aws_backup_restore_testing_plan` and `aws_backup_restore_testing_selection` to restore the EFS recovery points on a schedule, check that the SQLite file opens and passes `PRAGMA integrity_check`, and record the measured restore time against the RTO. Do a manual restore drill before you rely on the backups. (6) Add `aws_backup_vault_notifications` or EventBridge alarms for failed backup, copy and restore jobs.'}

LLM triage: uncertain — The Evidence shows an EFS file system (`juice_shop_data`, tagged `sqlite-data`) and an ECS Fargate task definition. The Address model stores profile data such as UserId, fullName, mobileNum and streetAddress through Sequelize. This partly matches the positive pattern. But the excerpts are cut off, so two things the rule needs can't be checked. First, the task definition stops before any `volume`/`efs_volume_configuration` or `mountPoints` block, so there's no proof that this EFS is the volume that holds the SQLite database. Second, the Evidence covers only parts of `networking.tf` and `main.tf`, so it can't show that no `aws_backup_plan`/`aws_backup_selection` or `aws_efs_backup_policy` exists for this file system. The negative example shows that such a backup resource would make this a false positive. Without the mount link and without confirming there's no backup anywhere in the Terraform, a missing-backup gap under Rule 6(1)(d) can't be confirmed or ruled out.; cache key: 223250c5caf1baee485b1f007bbeb4fbc967e98cfe3be71d71300460d863a34a

Without a backup, the Data Fiduciary has no way to keep processing this data if it is lost or becomes inaccessible, which Rule 6(1)(d) specifically requires. Recommended fix: Erase Personal Data once its Purpose is served, by wiring erasure into account-closure and end-of-purpose events rather than leaving the records in place. §8(7)(a) is the erasure duty this Issue Type cites for that case. Where Rule 8(1) applies to a Third Schedule class, schedule the erasure job from the retention period the Third Schedule sets. Tailored: {'fix': "With `paranoid: true`, Sequelize does a soft delete. `destroy()` only fills in `deletedAt` and leaves the personal data in the table, so closing an account doesn't erase anything as §8(7)(a) requires. To fix it: (1) For tables that hold personal data, set `paranoid: false`, or keep soft delete only as a short undo window. (2) In the account-closure and end-of-purpose handlers, call `Model.destroy({ where: { userId }, force: true })` on this model and on every associated model that holds personal data, all inside one transaction. Use `onDelete: 'CASCADE'` on the associations or delete the children explicitly. Where a record has to stay for legal or audit reasons, overwrite its personal fields with null or a non-reversible token instead of keeping the real values. (3) Add a scheduled purge job, such as a daily cron, that runs `Model.destroy({ where: { deletedAt: { [Op.lt]: cutoff } }, force: true, paranoid: false })`. Set the cutoff from the documented purpose-retention period. If Rule 8(1) applies to a Third Schedule class, use the retention period the Third Schedule sets for that class, and erase once it ends after the user's last interaction. Send the 48-hour pre-erasure notice that Rule 8(2) requires. (4) Remove the data from backups, caches, search indexes and processors too, log each erasure without storing the erased personal data, and add a test that checks the rows are physically gone after closure and after the purge runs."}

LLM triage: uncertain — The Evidence shows that the User model, which stores the email field (Contact data), sets `paranoid: true` (models/user.ts:120). With that setting, Sequelize's `destroy()` only marks a row as deleted by setting `deletedAt`, unless the call passes `force: true`. The record stays in the database. The rule needs one more thing to confirm a finding: a `destroy()` call on this model without `force: true`, especially on an erasure path. The only erasure Evidence is the start of the GET handler in routes/dataErasure.ts:24-66. It looks up the logged-in user and their email, then starts a SecurityAnswerModel query before the excerpt cuts off. It contains no `User.destroy()` call, with or without `force`, and no code that erases data. The negative example shows that a paranoid model alone is not a violation if deletion is forced or never happens on that path. Nothing in the Evidence shows how an erasure request is actually handled, for example a POST handler, a soft delete, or just logging a request, so it is unclear whether retention is overstated. If the erasure path does soft-delete Users, the email would be kept after consent is withdrawn, which would conflict with Section 8(7)(a). The Evidence shown does not establish that.; cache key: 6cd9d9a1a2440762f4a4a0f15bd0c48a172aabebdb39a1ea67e9bce16a6b7006

Recommended fix: Make the erasure route delete the Personal Data Elements from every store, cache and backup in scope, rather than setting a flag or hiding the records. Include the Data Processors in each erasure request, as §8(7)(b) requires, and confirm completion for each one. Keep only what a legal retention duty obliges you to keep, and erase the rest on request under §12(3). Tailored: {'fix': "Don't stop at `deletionRequested: true` in the erasure handler. Setting a flag or soft-deleting the record is not erasure. The handler should permanently delete or irreversibly anonymise the principal's personal data in every store in scope: primary DB tables, read replicas, caches (e.g. Redis/CDN), search indexes, analytics/log stores and backups. For backups that can't be edited, either expire the data on a short, documented rotation or use crypto-shredding, which deletes the per-user encryption key. Send the erasure request to every Data Processor that holds the data (§8(7)(b)), via its API or DPA channel, and record a confirmation of completion from each one. Before erasing, check each data element against a retention-policy map. Keep only the fields a specific legal duty requires (e.g. tax or accounting records), store them separately with restricted access, and set a deletion date when that duty ends. Erase everything else on request (§12(3)). Keep an audit record (request ID, timestamp, stores purged, processor confirmations, fields retained and the legal basis) that holds no erased personal data. Report the request as complete only when every store and processor has confirmed. Add a test that runs an erasure and then checks the DB, cache, search index and processor stubs for any remaining records."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The POST /dataerasure handler in routes/dataErasure.ts (lines 74-132) checks that the user is logged in, then only calls PrivacyRequestModel.create({ UserId: loggedInUser.data.id, deletionRequested: true }). This records that the user asked for deletion. It does not erase anything. In the code shown, nothing calls UserModel.destroy, anonymizes the user, or removes the email address from the Users table. The data flow shows the email is collected at registration (register.component.html, then POST /api/Users), stored in models/user.ts, and later returned in responses (routes/changePassword.ts, routes/saveLoginIp.ts), so it stays in the system after the request. This matches the positive RET-02 example, which only logs a deletion request, and differs from the negative example, which also calls User.destroy. Section 12(3) requires the Data Fiduciary to erase the data once it receives the request, unless the law or the specified purpose requires keeping it. The code gives no such reason and has no retention logic. Caveat: the handler excerpt is cut off after the create call. If a later part of lines 74-132 deletes or anonymizes the data, this would be a false positive. In the standard version of this handler (OWASP Juice Shop), the rest only clears the session cookie and renders a confirmation page.; cache key: b4cff2f9710f3f521c9c9aa8ae4145cc040ea0aa6cf9babe0b60acc278abfa94

Recommended fix: For a Third Schedule class, send the Data Principal a notice at least 48 hours before erasure under Rule 8(2), telling them the erasure is due and how to stop it. Build the notice into the erasure job so that it always runs ahead of deletion and cannot be skipped. Apply the requirement only where the Third Schedule class condition is met, not by default. Tailored: {'fix': "Don't delete straight away from this handler. Have it record a scheduled erasure that a single erasure job carries out, and build the Rule 8(2) notice into that job only when the Third Schedule class condition is met. (1) Add fields to PrivacyRequestModel: thirdScheduleClass (boolean), noticeSentAt, scheduledErasureAt, cancelledAt and status (pending_notice \| notified \| cancelled \| erased). (2) In the POST handler, create the request with deletionRequested: true, and set thirdScheduleClass by calling a central helper such as isThirdScheduleClass(dataFiduciary, purpose). Base that helper on config for the entity class (e-commerce, online gaming or social media intermediary over the user threshold) and the purpose. Don't hard-code it to true. Set status to 'pending_notice' if the condition is met. Otherwise follow the existing erasure path unchanged, so the 48-hour notice isn't applied by default. (3) In the erasure job, for Third Schedule records: if noticeSentAt is null, send the Data Principal a notice (email or registered contact) saying their personal data will be erased on scheduledErasureAt, and explaining how to stop it, for example by logging in, using the account, or a cancel link or endpoint. Save noticeSentAt, set scheduledErasureAt to at least noticeSentAt + 48 hours, set status to 'notified', and don't delete anything in that run. Delete only when status is 'notified', noticeSentAt is set, now &gt;= noticeSentAt + 48h, and cancelledAt is null. Check this inside the delete function itself, so no other code path can skip the notice. If sending the notice fails, keep the record pending and retry, and never fall through to deletion. (4) Add a cancel route (for example POST /erasure/cancel/:id, authenticated or with a signed token) that sets cancelledAt and status 'cancelled'. Also treat any login or activity by the Data Principal before scheduledErasureAt as cancelling the erasure. (5) Keep an audit log of the notice's content, when it was sent, the delivery channel, and the erasure or cancellation outcome. (6) Add tests: Third Schedule records are never erased without a notice sent at least 48h earlier; a cancellation stops erasure; non-Third Schedule records follow the normal flow with no notice gate."}

LLM triage: false-positive — The code shown (routes/dataErasure.ts:74-132) handles a request the user starts herself. An authenticated user submits an erasure request, and the handler only records it with PrivacyRequestModel.create({ UserId, deletionRequested: true }). Nothing in the visible code deletes data right away, and there is no inactivity-based erasure job. Rule 8(2) requires a 48-hour notice only before erasure that happens because a Third Schedule retention period runs out while the user is inactive. Rule 8(1) and 8(2) both treat a user exercising her rights as a reason that erasure is not triggered by inactivity. Since this deletion is user-requested, the 48-hour pre-erasure notice does not apply. The evidence also doesn't show that the Fiduciary falls under the Third Schedule (for example, an e-commerce entity with at least two crore registered users). The excerpt is cut off, but the visible logic doesn't match the positive pattern: there is no direct destroy without notice.; cache key: 5c7ffbc0dd5fa9b71b54e1e1765e3130eb3bfebb4c7ada85e1bbc3ffd3cd396c

Recommended fix: Give each Data Principal a summary of the Personal Data Elements held about them and of the processing done on that data, as §11(1)(a) requires. Where a sharing flow exists, list the Data Fiduciaries and Data Processors the data was shared with and what was shared, under §11(1)(b). Serve the summary through an in-app or request route that Rule 14 describes. Tailored: {'fix': "Scope every query in dataExport to the authenticated user and stop trusting req.body.UserId. Right now any logged-in user can send a different UserId and get another person's memories, which breaks §11. Change it to MemoryModel.findAll({ where: { UserId: loggedInUser.data.id } }). Look up orders and reviews by the same server-side id or email too, never by values from the client. Return 401 if there is no valid «SECRET» Then expand the export into a §11(1)(a) summary. For each Personal Data Element held (username, email, profile image, addresses, payment cards, orders, reviews, memories, login IPs), list the category, its source, the purpose of processing, the processing done on it, the legal basis and how long it is kept. If any sharing flow exists (payment processor, delivery partner, analytics, etc.), add a §11(1)(b) section that names each Data Fiduciary or Data Processor, says which elements were shared, why and when, or states plainly that nothing was shared. Serve this from a dedicated authenticated route, for example GET /rest/user/data-export, linked from the account privacy page as Rule 14 describes. Log each request so you can show it was answered, and add a test that confirms user A cannot get user B's data by changing UserId."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The data-export endpoint (POST /rest/user/data-export, routes/dataExport.ts) only puts together the username, a masked email (vowels replaced with '*', so the real stored value isn't returned), memories, orders and reviews. The same Evidence shows the system also stores other personal data tied to the user: Card records (fullName, cardNum, expMonth, expYear), Complaint files, Feedback comments and ratings, and basket coupons. The visible export logic never queries these models. That is the incomplete-export pattern in the positive example, where data is stored but left out of the export. So the user's data access request returns only part of a summary of the personal data being processed. One caveat: the handler excerpt is cut off ('…'), so the exact final response object isn't shown. Still, nothing in the visible code suggests cards, complaints or feedback get fetched later. Recommended fix: Give each Data Principal a route to correct, complete or update every user-editable Personal Data Element, and apply the change in every store the value lives in, including caches and derived records. §12(2) provides the right to correction, completion and update, and Rule 14 sets how the Data Principal exercises it. Tailored: {'fix': "Make updateUserProfile a complete correction route under DPDP §12(2) and Rule 14, so the Data Principal can correct, complete or update every personal data element they're allowed to edit. The visible code only looks up the user by loggedInUser.data.id, so it doesn't yet show that every field can be changed or that changes reach every store. (1) Accept and validate each editable field, not just username. Use a strict allow-list such as username, email, profileImage and other contact details. Reject unknown fields, check formats and uniqueness (for example, email), and never let role, id, isActive, totpSecret or the password hash be changed through this route. (2) Look up the user only by the authenticated identity, as the code already does, and require CSRF protection or a same-site check. (3) Save the changes inside a database transaction, and in the same transaction update every derived or copied record that holds the old value. Examples are stored copies of the email or username in reviews, complaints, feedback, orders, deliveries, addresses and memories. Where possible, change those records to point to the user ID instead of keeping their own copy. (4) Update the caches: replace the security.authenticatedUsers entry for the current token, issue a new JWT with the corrected claims via security.authorize(updatedUser), set the new token cookie, and invalidate any other active sessions or cached profile and token data, so no old value keeps being served. (5) Return the updated profile so the user can confirm the change was applied. Record the correction request and its result for Rule 14 accountability. Reply within the required timeframe, and if a field can't be changed, tell the user why. (6) Add tests showing that each editable field can be corrected, that the change appears in the database, the session cache, the reissued token and all derived records, and that protected fields can't be changed."}

LLM triage: true-positive — The Evidence points to no working way for a user to correct their stored email address. The generic update route for the user record, PUT /api/Users/:id (server.ts:396-398), is blocked by security.denyAll(), so the auto-generated finale endpoint cannot be used to change a user's email. Email is taken at registration (POST /api/Users, server.ts:440-454) and written through the User model's email setter (models/user.ts:56-69). The only other self-service profile route is POST /profile (server.ts:707), handled by updateUserProfile. The part of that handler shown here only checks who is logged in and loads the user record; nothing shown reads req.body.email or passes email to user.update. This matches the positive example, where the profile route updates only username, rather than the negative one, which updates email. The other POST routes listed (profile image upload, Complaints, Recycles, Feedbacks) don't touch email. Caveat: the updateUserProfile excerpt is cut off before its update call, so this verdict relies on the blocked PUT route plus no email handling anywhere in the Evidence. Even so, the data flow shows email being collected, stored and exposed with no correction path, which falls short of the right to correct and update personal data.; cache key: 4fe7819d8304839ca4ebdd9d258dcb5312fa30cb8ed0ba8f316f995ce70c7fd0

LLM reasoning: None of the seven consent criteria can be determined from the supplied Evidence. The graph has one cookie consent banner (banner:frontend/src/index.html:19), which only shows window.cookieconsent.initialise. It also has several Privacy Policy notice paragraphs and a GDPR erasure notice. Every other part is empty: no purposes, grant paths, controls, enforcement points, records, withdrawal paths, processors, processor propagation or operations. Each notice has before_grant null, purpose_ids empty, and itemised and language null, so no notice is linked to any consent request or purpose. One notice treats 'By using the Service, you agree to the collection and use of information', and a later 'consent to this Privacy Policy', as agreement. That reads as implied, bundled consent, which raises a concern under Section 6(1), but there is no link to a purpose or grant path that would support a finding. The Evidence says missing links are not proof of absence, and the instruction is not to infer missing links, so each criterion is marked not determinable rather than not met.; cache key: ce10a5826acfbf7a2216bc40aadee1be575437e372ee70409d3b9534d96df59f

## Statutory Penalty Ceilings and Enforcement Timeline

Statutory maximum per Schedule; not a predicted or estimated fine. The actual penalty is determined by the Data Protection Board under section 33(2) factors. Ceilings apply per breach instance and are never added together. Under the Report's Applicability Assumptions, only applicable Primary Provisions contribute to the highest ceiling. Related and conditional Provisions and Findings that need legal review do not contribute.

Highest applicable ceiling: ₹250 crore (DPDP-Rule-6(1)(a), DPDP-Rule-6(1)(b), DPDP-Rule-6(1)(c), DPDP-Rule-6(1)(d), DPDP-Rule-6(1)(e)). Ceilings are not summed into a total exposure figure.
